Whether reuse happens is visible on any machine without a debugger: winload logs every attempt as event 45 in `Microsoft-Windows-Kernel-Boot/Operational`, "VSM Identity Key Provisioning. Unsealing cached copy status: ... New key generation status: ... Measuring to PCR status: ... Sealing and caching status: ...". On my machine the first four boots after an OS upgrade all failed to unseal and generated a new key, the first with `0xC000000F` (no cached file yet) and the next three with `0xC0280018`. That value is `TPM_E_WRONGPCRVAL` (0x80280018, the error for a PCR value that does not match the sealed one) after the status conversion winload applies, which clears bit 28 and sets bit 30. (The boot numbers here and in the figure are the numbers of the Measured Boot log files, not the boot counter measured in PCR 12.)

To find out what changed, I diffed the TCG logs of consecutive boots, PCR by PCR, over the masked set. Between the first boots the partition table (PCR 5), the Secure Boot `dbx` variable (PCR 7, a revocation update had just been applied) and the boot-module and boot-authority measurements (PCRs 13 and 14) changed. Between the next pair, only one thing differed in the masked PCRs. The trust-boundary event in PCR 13 contained `SIPAEVENT_VBS_HVCI_POLICY = 1` in the first boot and not in the second, because Memory Integrity had been on and then was not. Turning HVCI on or off is enough to rotate the signing key.

That cleanest pair was an accident. I had turned Memory Integrity on for the earlier experiments and rebooted to test persistence, and the report call failed with `ERROR_NOT_SUPPORTED`: HVCI was off again. A local Group Policy on my machine set "Turn On Virtualization Based Security" to Disabled. As far as I can tell from the timing, Device Guard re-applied it early in every boot and wrote `Enabled = 0` back to the HVCI setting, so the Windows Security toggle only ever lasted one boot. Annoying, but it gave me two consecutive boots whose sealed PCRs differed in exactly one thing.

![Signing key rotation across six boots](/assets/img/posts/rtattest-key-rotation.svg)
_Six consecutive boots on the real machine. Boot 479 had no cached key yet, after that the key is regenerated whenever a masked PCR changes, and only boot 484, where no masked PCR changed, unsealed the cached copy and reused the previous boot's key._

### 5.5 Replaying a Package From an Earlier Boot

Then the positive test. I turned HVCI back on and rebooted: the key changed again, as predicted, because PCR 13 regained the HVCI event. In that boot I requested a package with a fresh nonce and rebooted again with the same configuration (I only re-armed the HVCI setting the policy had reset, as before). This time event 45 reported `Unsealing cached copy status: 0x0`, with no new key generated and nothing resealed. The measured IDKS was byte-identical to the previous boot's. And the package from the previous boot verified with the new boot's measured key: all 1479 checks of my parser passed.

The boot after HVCI was re-enabled had masked PCRs identical to a boot two reboots earlier. It still did not get that boot's key back. Winload reads and rewrites a single cached payload, so as far as I can tell only the most recently sealed copy survives, and returning to an earlier configuration produces a third key. And a package requested in the later boot with the same nonce as the earlier one is just as valid; the two differ only in driver load order and one `Unloaded` flag.

Putting the code and these six boots together: on bare metal in HW mode the IDKS lives as long as the masked PCRs stay the same, which on a stable machine can be many boots. I observed the reuse once, from boot 483 to 484; the rule itself comes from the code. Firmware updates, `dbx` updates, partition changes, boot driver changes and VBS policy changes rotate it. Nothing in a package says which boot produced it, so a package signed in an earlier boot verifies with the current boot's key whenever the key survived. The key also survives hibernation, and on a Kernel Soft Reboot the old Secure Kernel exports it for its successor (section 8).

---

## 6. Where Driver Loads Are Recorded

The report is only as good as the list behind it, so the next question is when a driver gets into that list. There is exactly one way in: `SkmmPrepareImageRelocations` is the only caller of `SkmiAddSecureImageToNtTable`, which is the only caller of `SkAddRuntimeDriverToNtTable`. `SkmmPrepareImageRelocations` is the VTL1 handler of secure service 0x1D, which the PDB calls `SECURESERVICE_PREPARE_IMAGE_RELOCATIONS`.

### 6.1 Who Sends 0x1D

The interesting part is who sends 0x1D. The NT kernel never calls `nt!VslPrepareSecureImageRelocations` directly; it is slot +0x40 of a table called `VslHvciInterface`, which NT hands to `ci.dll` at initialization. CI copies the table, and the only reader of that slot is `ci!CiHvciTransferRelocationInformation`, which CI installs into a validation context only when the image is validated in VTL1. It is called from `CipValidateFileHash` and `CipValidatePageHash`, and only once the hash and signature checks succeeded:

```c
// ci!CipValidateFileHash (26100.9711)
if ( FirstMatch >= 0 ) {
  v23 = *(...)(*(_QWORD *)(a1 + 2760) + 104LL);   // slot +0x68: TransferRelocationInformation -> 0x1D
  if ( v23 != nullptr ) FirstMatch = v23(a1, a2: a4, a3: a5);
  ...
```

On the NT side that validation runs while the driver's image section is created: `MmLoadSystemImageEx` calls `MiObtainSectionForDriver`, whose section creation ends in `MiValidateSectionCreate` and the CI callback `CiValidateImageHeader`. `IopLoadDriver` calls `MmLoadSystemImageEx` before `PnpCallDriverEntry`. Put together, a runtime driver is recorded after VTL1 has validated its signature and before it is relocated to its system address, before its imports are resolved, and before `DriverEntry` runs.

```
nt!IopLoadDriver
  nt!MmLoadSystemImageEx
    nt!MiObtainSectionForDriver -> ... -> nt!MiValidateSectionCreate
      ci!CiValidateImageHeader -> ci!CipValidateFileHash / CipValidatePageHash   (VTL1 validation)
        ci!CiHvciTransferRelocationInformation -> nt!VslPrepareSecureImageRelocations
          secure call 0x1D -> securekernel!SkmmPrepareImageRelocations
            securekernel!SkmiAddSecureImageToNtTable -> SkAddRuntimeDriverToNtTable
  nt!PnpCallDriverEntry
```

The VTL1 handler refuses images that were not validated (flag 0x20 on the image object, which `SkmmFinishSecureImageValidation` sets only after `skci!SkciFinishImageValidation` succeeded), and recording is its last step:

```c
// securekernel!SkmmPrepareImageRelocations (26100.9711)
v8 = SkmiPrepareImageRelocations(a1: v13, a2: (__int64)v20);
if ( v8 >= 0 ) { ...
  v16 = SkmiAddSecureImageToNtTable(a1: v13);
  v8 = v16;
  if ( v16 == -1073741275 || v16 == 0x40000000 || v16 == -1073741432 )  // NOT_FOUND, NAME_EXISTS, 0xC0000188
    v8 = 0;
```

Three results are turned into success: no attestation data for this image, image hash already recorded, and table full. So a driver loads normally in all three cases, and in the first and last it loads without being listed. An allocation failure, on the other hand, fails the secure call and the load. Two consequences follow. First, the record means "passed VTL1 validation", not "ran": a driver whose load fails after this point, in its imports or in `DriverEntry`, stays recorded. Second, the one success exit that skips both relocation preparation and the insert is "this image was already prepared"; I first suspected the load-config branches offered another, but they cannot succeed without recording.

### 6.2 The HVCI Gate

Learn says the report requires HVCI, and Section 3 showed that nothing in VTL0 checks it. The check is in the recording path:

```c
// securekernel!SkmmInitSystem phase 0 (26100.9711)
v16 = *(_DWORD *)(SkeLoaderBlock + 1440) & 1;            // loader block +0x5A0, bit 0
SkmiFlags = SkmiFlags & 0xFFFFFFCF | (16 * v16);         // -> SkmiFlags 0x10
// securekernel!SkmiAddSecureImageToNtTable
if ( (SkmiFlags & 0x10) == 0 )
  return 0;                                              // nothing recorded
```

`SkmiFlags & 0x10` is the bit the Secure Kernel reports to NT and CI as "HVCI enabled", through secure service 0xFB (`SECURESERVICE_QUERY_STRONG_CODE_FEATURES`). CI turns it into `g_CiOptions & 0x8000` and, apart from a few special-case flags, only forwards kernel images to VTL1 validation when it is set. With VBS on but HVCI off, no runtime driver is ever recorded, the table stays empty, and `SkpBuildNtDriversReport` returns `STATUS_NOT_SUPPORTED`. On my machine with VBS on and Memory Integrity off, every well-formed request failed with `ERROR_NOT_SUPPORTED` (50), and the raw syscall returned `STATUS_NOT_SUPPORTED`. I traced winload for that `+0x5A0` bit: `OslpVsmInitializeLoaderBlock` sets it in one place, from the boot-time HVCI level in the VSM policy, so it is 1 exactly when HVCI is on for the boot. NT never sees that secure-kernel block, but winload copies the same policy value into the loader extension's `IumPolicy` field, which NT does read. I confirmed that live: a kernel debugger attached to the HVCI-on lab VM at boot read `IumPolicy` as `0x283`, whose HVCI-level bits (`0x180`) are `0x80`, that is level 1, HVCI on.

One more filter: `skci` computes the signer thumbprint only for images whose type flags include bit 0 or bit 2, the mask CI uses for kernel-mode images. Without a thumbprint there is no attestation data, the insert returns "not found", and the image is skipped silently. Only kernel-type images can enter the table.

---

## 7. The Driver Table and the Driver Report

### 7.1 The Table

The list lives in a VTL1 hash table rooted at `SkpNtDriversTable`, initialized once per boot by `SkmmInitSystem` with a cap of 384 entries. That cap is the SDK's `MaximumDriversRecorded`, which no header defines. Each entry is 88 bytes of zeroed VTL1 pool (tag `NtDr`). The PDB has no type for it, so the fields are offsets:

| offset | size | content |
|---|---|---|
| +0x00 | 8 | bucket chain link |
| +0x08 | 8 | bucket key: first 8 bytes of the image hash |
| +0x10 | 4 | low WORD LoadCount; bit 30 HotPatch; bit 31 Unloaded; bit 29 (BootDriver) never written |
| +0x14 | 4 | insertion index |
| +0x18 | 4 | image hash ALG_ID |
| +0x1C | 4 | thumbprint ALG_ID |
| +0x20 | 8 | pointer to the image hash |
| +0x28 | 8 | pointer to the 20-byte SHA-1 thumbprint |
| +0x30 | 16 | `UNICODE_STRING` InternalName |
| +0x40 | 16 | `UNICODE_STRING` OEM (OPUS program) name |
| +0x50 | 8 | file version, kept but never serialized |

Section 15.4 walks this structure in live VTL1 memory and checks every field in the table above against it.

The key is the image hash and nothing else. `SkAddRuntimeDriverToNtTable` takes the table lock exclusively and looks the hash up. On a hit it only increments LoadCount, saturating at 0xFFFF. Name, thumbprint and ALG_ID are not compared, so one file loaded under two names is one entry. On a miss it appends a new entry if the count is below 384, and otherwise returns `0xC0000188`, which, as shown above, the caller swallows.

```c
// securekernel!SkAddRuntimeDriverToNtTable (26100.9711)
RtlAcquireSRWLockExclusive(a1: &SkpNtDriversTable);
v7 = SkpLookupNtDriverEntryByImageHash(Source2: *(void **)(a1 + 32), Length: v4);
if ( v7 != 0 ) {
  if ( (unsigned __int16)*(_DWORD *)(v7 + 16) != 0xFFFF ) ++*(_WORD *)(v7 + 16);   // LoadCount
  v8 = 0x40000000;                                           // Unloaded bit untouched
} else {
  ...
  if ( (unsigned int)qword_1401324B8 < dword_1401324C0 ) {   // count < 384
    if ( SkpGrowDriversHashTableAndInsert(a1) ) { ... v8 = 0; }
    else v8 = -1073741670;
  } else v8 = -1073741432;                                   // 0xC0000188, table full
}
```

Nothing ever removes an entry. I enumerated every function that touches the table: entries are appended, LoadCount grows, the Unloaded bit gets set, the chain link is rewritten when the bucket array grows, and that is all. This is the real meaning of "append-only": a VTL1 list that is never pruned for the lifetime of a boot, not a hash chain in the TPM (section 9).

### 7.2 What Is Recorded

Start with the image hash. `SkmmFinishSecureImageValidation` captures it right after `skci` validated the image and keeps it on the image object; the table entry copies it. Which digest that is depends on how `skci` validated the image. On the file-hash path it is the Authenticode digest computed in VTL1 and checked against the signature or catalog. For embedded page-hash signatures it is the Authenticode digest the signer asserted in the signature. On the catalog page-hash path I found no instruction that writes the field, which would leave it zero; on my machine that never happened. I hashed every loaded driver file myself. 173 report entries equal the Authenticode PE hash of a loaded file, 77 of them catalog-signed inbox drivers without an embedded signature, and two more equal the files behind loaded crash-dump copies. The remaining eight belong to drivers that were no longer loaded. No entry had an all-zero hash. The algorithm is per image: 105 entries were SHA-256 and 78 SHA-1.

The signer thumbprint is a SHA-1 hash of the leaf certificate of the last signer entry, computed in VTL1. It is always SHA-1, whatever the image hash algorithm. For a driver signed through Microsoft's hardware program, that leaf is Microsoft's certificate, not the vendor's. The `vgk.sys` entry on my machine carries the thumbprint of "Microsoft Windows Hardware Compatibility Publisher", although the file also has Riot's own signature. The vendor shows up in the OEM name instead.

The OEM name is the program name from the signature's authenticated OPUS attribute. `skci` extracts it only when `CiCompareSigningLevels(signer level, 12)` returns 0, and drops it if it equals "Microsoft Windows" (case-insensitive); the SDK says inbox drivers have none. On my machine 35 entries carried one, for example `vgk.sys` with "Riot Games".

`InternalName` needed more care, because Trehan's hotpatch post says the hotpatch report's image name is queried from VTL0. For the driver report it is not VTL0 text. CI sends the image's version resource to VTL1 (secure service 0x21). `skci!SkciTransferVersionResource` stores it only if its file offset lies inside the resource range VTL1 derived from the image headers, and only if the active code integrity policy has a particular bit set. VTL1 then parses `InternalName` out of it, and `SkciFinishImageValidation` checks those bytes against the image's signed page hashes. A mismatch fails the image. So VTL0 cannot inject arbitrary text. It is not fully VTL1-chosen either. VTL0 picks which signed version structure inside the resource range VTL1 parses, signers with a particular flag skip the page check, and the name is simply empty when the policy bit is clear. Two entries on my machine had an empty name. Do not key on it.

### 7.3 Serializing the Report

`SkpBuildNtDriversReport` holds the table lock shared for the whole walk, so entries cannot be added halfway through a report. The walk follows hash buckets, but each entry is written into the slot given by its insertion index, so `DriverEntries[]` comes out in load order. The fields map directly:

| `DRIVER_INFO_ENTRY` field | source |
|---|---|
| `InternalName[32]` | entry +0x30, converted to UTF-8; 32 bytes copied with no NUL, longer names cut to 31 bytes |
| `ImageHashAlgorithm` | entry +0x18; hash length from the ALG_ID (SHA-1 20, SHA-256 32, SHA-384 48, SHA-512 64, anything else 0) |
| `PublisherThumbprintHashAlgorithm` | entry +0x1C, always `CALG_SHA1` |
| `ImageHashOffset`, `PublisherThumbprintOffset` | offsets of the bytes in the report's dynamic area |
| `LoadCount` | low WORD of entry +0x10 |
| `OemNameSize`, `OemNameOffset` | entry +0x40 as UTF-8 plus a NUL, if present |
| `Flags` | Unloaded = bit 31, BootDriver = bit 29, HotPatch = bit 30 of entry +0x10 |

And one real entry: `vgk.sys`, Vanguard's own driver, the fifth entry of the package above.

```
00000278  76 67 6b 2e 73 79 73 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00   InternalName "vgk.sys"
00000288  00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00 00
00000298  0c 80 04 80 cb 2a 00 00 eb 2a 00 00 01 00 0a 00   CALG_SHA_256, CALG_SHA1, hash at 0x2acb, thumbprint at 0x2aeb,
                                                            LoadCount 1, OEM name 10 bytes
000002a8  ff 2a 00 00 00 00 00 00                           OEM name at 0x2aff, Flags 0, Padding 0

image hash : 7f9c9d6bc6b08440727733fd34641c76cccaa43f8291f7bdc659129134236a70
thumbprint : 6022340c9f4cb00307df8db036d392adac766212
OEM name   : "Riot Games"
```

In the report header, `NumberOfDrivers` is the number of entries walked. `ReportOverflowed` is set when the count reached 384, so it means "table full" rather than "something was dropped". `PartialReport` is always 0. `IncludeBootDrivers` would be 1 only if some entry had the BootDriver bit, and nothing sets it. The snapshot is not perfectly consistent: `SkmiFreeNar` sets the Unloaded bit with an interlocked operation after its lookup released the lock, so a flag can change while a report is being serialized.

On my machine the first report had 183 entries in load order, none with BootDriver or HotPatch set, and all three report flags at 0.

---

## 8. Unload, Reload, Boot Drivers, Hotpatch, Hibernate, and Soft Reboot

### 8.1 Unload

When a driver is unloaded, `nt!MiUnloadSystemImage` calls `MiReleasePrivilegedPtes`, which issues secure call 0xD3 through `VslReserveProtectedPages`. That flag-gated release path is active on any VBS system. In VTL1 the request reaches `SkmiFreeNar` for the image's address range, which marks the table entry:

```c
// securekernel!SkmiFreeNar (26100.9711)
if ( (*(_BYTE *)(a1 + 94) & 6) != 2 ) goto LABEL_71;          // image NAR only
if ( (SkmiFlags & 0x10) != 0 ) {
  v14 = *(_QWORD *)(a1 + 56);                                  // image object
  if ( v14 != 0 ) {
    v15 = SkpLookupNtDriverEntryByImageHash(*(v14 + 192), *(v14 + 200), a3: 0);
    if ( v15 != 0 ) _InterlockedCompareExchange(v15 + 16, v17 | 0x80000000, v17);   // Unloaded
```

Nothing else changes: no decrement, no removal. A BYOVD driver that is loaded, used and unloaded stays in every report for the rest of the boot with `Unloaded = 1`, which is the property an anti-cheat wants. The marking is per image hash, though, so if one file is loaded twice under two names, unloading one marks the shared entry.

### 8.2 Reload, and Why Unloaded Does Not Mean Unloaded

On 26100.7705 through 26100.9711, the duplicate path in `SkAddRuntimeDriverToNtTable` only increments LoadCount and never clears the Unloaded bit. A driver that is loaded, unloaded and loaded again therefore stays `Unloaded = 1` while it runs. 28000.3231 changes this: its version clears bit 31 when an image hash is recorded again. That only helps reloads that record the hash again, and the next paragraph shows that a reload does not always do so.

The experiment showed it is worse than that, though. I loaded the Microsoft-signed Process Explorer driver: it was appended at the end of the list with LoadCount 1. I unloaded it, and its entry got `Unloaded = 1`. I loaded it again, and the driver was running, but the entry still said `Unloaded = 1` and LoadCount stayed 1. I had predicted LoadCount 2. The static path explains why. When a driver is unloaded, NT can keep its image section cached rather than delete it; on the next load `MiReferenceControlArea` reuses that cached control area, and `ci.dll` finds the image already validated under it, so it does not send secure service 0x1D again. LoadCount is bumped only when 0x1D records an image hash, so a cached reload leaves it at 1. The secure image is closed only when the control area is finally deleted, through `CiReleaseContext`. LoadCount therefore counts VTL1 validations, not loads. The lab VM backs up the other half: after the driver was unloaded, deleting the file and copying the same bytes back forced a fresh validation that bumped the same hash entry to LoadCount 2, still with `Unloaded = 1`, and no new entry was added. (That VM run also had a cached-reload step, but the guest restarted during it, so it shows nothing; the cached reload result is the one from my desktop above.) The first report on my desktop already had six loaded drivers flagged Unloaded, all with LoadCount 1; three of them are the in-memory crash-dump copies (`dump_stornvme.sys`, `dump_dumpstorport.sys` and `dump_dumpfve.sys`), which the report lists under their original names. After an S3 sleep the Xbox controller driver's entry got the flag as well, most likely because its driver was unloaded and loaded again across the sleep transition; I did not trace that.

The practical rule: `Unloaded = 1` means "was unloaded at least once since boot", never "is not loaded now". A verifier must not drop such entries.

### 8.3 Boot Drivers

Drivers loaded by winload are never inserted. The boot-driver code in VTL1 (`SkmiConfigureBootDriverPages`, `SECURESERVICE_REGISTER_BOOT_DRIVERS`) calls no table function, and nothing writes the BootDriver bit, so `IncludeBootDrivers` is always 0. That is the SDK's "in that case the information is stored in the TCG Log" branch, and it is the only one that exists.

The experiment makes the split concrete. Of the 254 kernel modules loaded on my machine, 172 were covered only by the report and 78 only by winload's module measurements in the TCG log. One, `stornvme.sys`, was covered by both, and only because its crash-dump copy, recorded under the original name, has the same hash. Three could not be matched to either source: the in-memory crash-dump copies (`dump_*.sys`) have no file of their own to hash, although the report does carry them under their original names with `Unloaded` set. Drivers loaded by winload, such as `Ntfs.sys`, `tcpip.sys` and `tpm.sys`, were absent from the report and present in the boot log, including the ones registered only as demand-start (`Ntfs.sys`, `tpm.sys`, `msrpc.sys`). Neither source alone covers the loaded set.

### 8.4 Hotpatches

As far as I can tell from the code, the HotPatch flag marks a patch image, not the driver it patches: it is set when the image's own load configuration carries hotpatch information (`HotPatchTableOffset`), which is what a patch image has. I did not check real files to confirm that a base driver never carries it. Applying or reapplying a patch never touches the table. I traced the full direct-call closure of every VTL1 hotpatch service (indirect calls not covered), and none of them reach a table function, so a patched driver's entry does not change. The patch image itself takes the ordinary driver path: `ci.dll` validates it and sends secure service 0x1D, so on an HVCI system it should get its own entry, keyed by the patch's own hash, with HotPatch set and no OEM name (the signing-level check a patch must pass to be applied is the opposite result of the one OEM-name capture needs). The one piece I could not pin down is the exact validation flags CI passes for a patch image, so this last step is an inference, not a traced fact, and I could not load a real driver hotpatch to confirm it.

### 8.5 Sleep and Hibernate

The table is initialized only at boot, and neither the hibernate path nor the resume path touches it, so it survives as long as VTL1 memory does. On my machine, the report after hibernate and resume listed the same 192 entries with the same hashes, LoadCounts, flags and order, and its signature verified with the key measured in the original boot. S3 sleep behaved the same, apart from the one controller driver that got flagged Unloaded.

There is a verifier trap here, and I tested it on the real TPM. Resuming from hibernation starts a new Measured Boot log segment (`<boot>-0000000001.log`), and that segment contains no IDKS event: the key is measured only in the boot segment. A verifier that reads "the newest log" finds no key. Worse, the current quote does not cover the boot measurement either. I read PCR 12 (where the IDKS is measured), hibernated the machine, and read it again after resume: it had changed, from `66e57fa6...` to `205d8d11...`, and the update counter had jumped. Hibernation powers the TPM off, resume re-measures the PCRs from zero, and the resume path never re-logs the IDKS. Replaying the two segments against the post-resume PCRs makes it explicit: the boot segment, which holds the IDKS event, no longer matches the live PCR 12, while the resume segment, which has no IDKS, matches exactly. So a TPM quote taken after a resume cannot anchor the signing key at all. A verifier must use the boot-segment log and the boot-time quote, not the newest log and not a post-resume quote.

### 8.6 Kernel Soft Reboot

A Kernel Soft Reboot replaces the running kernel without a firmware reset, and it is the one transition where the signing key is set up to outlive the table. Before it halts, the old Secure Kernel copies a fixed set of nine slots out for its successor (`SkpKsrRebootNotify`), and slot 3 is the IDKS. The table is not in that set. Nothing registers it, and no driver-table function is reachable from any of the KSR entry points. How the successor picks the exported slots up is not visible in `securekernel.exe`.

The loading side sits in `skksrext.sys`, a Secure Kernel extension that Winbindex lists in the Windows 10 and Windows 11 24H2 update packages but that is not installed on my machine. That is why I first wrote this step off as untraced. Since Winbindex has the binary, being absent locally was no reason to skip it. One caveat: it is not indexed at 9711. The newest copy Winbindex has is the one first shipped in 10.0.26100.6725 (it stays unchanged through the December 2025 updates), so this subsection alone is from an older build than the rest of the post.

VTL0 drives the load through `KsrApiCall`, which dispatches to `KsrpApiLoadDriver`. From there:

```
KsrApiCall
  KsrpApiLoadDriver
    KsrLoadLoader
      KsrpSkLoadAndValidateImageData   open, read, allocate, bounds-check headers
      KsrpSkBindImageImports           resolve imports by name
      LdrRelocateImage
      KsrpSkCallDriverEntry            build a 0x1B0 loader block, call the entry point
```

`KsrpSkCallDriverEntry` reads `AddressOfEntryPoint` through `RtlImageNtHeaderEx`, allocates a 0x1B0-byte block with pool tag `KSR `, stores the image base and size in it, and calls the entry point with that block as its first argument.

Two things follow. First, the table cannot be reconstructed on the far side. `skksrext.sys` imports 77 functions from `securekernel` and not one of them touches attestation, reports or the driver table. Its only image-related imports are `SkmmPreRegisterHvImage`, `SkmmCommitPreRegisterHvImage` and `SkmmCancelPreRegisterHvImage`, and none of the three has a call site in the binary at all: each appears only in the import thunk table and the control-flow-guard tables. None of the 432 functions in the PDB is named for a hash, a signature, a catalog or an attestation.

Second, and I did not expect this: as far as I can tell, the image is not cryptographically validated. Despite the name, `KsrpSkLoadAndValidateImageData` calls file I/O and page allocation helpers, `RtlImageNtHeaderEx`, `memset`, and fifteen indirect calls through twelve slots of the Secure Kernel service table. The seven slots I could name are `ZwOpenFile`, `ZwQueryInformationFile`, `ZwClose`, `ExAllocatePoolWithTag`, `ExFreePoolWithTag`, `MmMapLockedPagesSpecifyCache` and `MmUnmapLockedPages`. The validation I can see is structural: it bounds-checks the PE headers and returns `STATUS_INVALID_IMAGE_FORMAT` when they do not fit. So this load does not pass through the `ci.dll` to secure service 0x1D to `SkmiAddSecureImageToNtTable` pipeline that section 6 describes, and the image it starts is not recorded in the table.

Five of those twelve slots I could not name, and I want to be exact about what that costs. Four of them have no name entry in the `.rdata` dynamic-import table and no thin wrapper carrying a PDB name; from the argument shapes they are an open, a query and a close over one handle, plus a read-like call that takes a length from the image context. The fifth is called once per section inside the section copy loop with a page-rounded size, which looks like a mapping or protection change, but that is a guess. If one of the five were a signature check I would have missed it, so read "not cryptographically validated" as bounded by those five.

What remains open is the next step out. `KsrLoadLoader` loads and starts a loader image, not a Secure Kernel. Whether that loader then brings up a fresh Secure Kernel is not visible in `skksrext.sys`, and I have no SKU that performs a KSR to watch it happen.

---

## 9. No TPM: Correcting "Append-Only Hash in the TPM"

Riot's post and the press coverage describe the feature as measuring every on-demand driver into the TPM, "a sort of continual, cumulative hash of all drivers that have been loaded since boot". I looked for that from every angle I could think of, and it is not there.

- `securekernel.exe` has 41 imports, from `skci`, `cng` and two kernel extension sets; `skci.dll` imports only from the Secure Kernel; `ntoskrnl.exe` imports nothing TPM-related.
- The `_SKSERVICE` enum has no TPM, PCR, TCG, SIPA or measurement service, so NT has no secure call with which to ask VTL1 to extend a PCR.
- The only TPM-named functions in the Secure Kernel belong to trustlet key binding. None of the functions matching `Tpm|Tbs|Tcg|Pcr|Measure|Sipa|Wbcl` appears within five calls of the recording, unload or report functions, and none of the NT matches is within four calls of `IopLoadDriver`, `MmLoadSystemImageEx` or `MiUnloadSystemImage`.
- `SkProduceRuntimeSignedReport` itself calls no TPM function.

So runtime driver loads are not measured into the TPM. They are recorded in a VTL1 table that is never pruned and exported in a package signed with the IDKS. The TPM is involved only at boot. Winload seals the secret protecting the stored IDKS to PCR values and measures the key's public half into the TCG log, which is what lets a remote verifier trust the key. The security property Riot describes (the list cannot be rewritten after the fact by a kernel attacker) does hold, but it comes from VTL1 isolation and the signature, not from the TPM.

---

## 10. History: An Empty Signed Report, Then a Table, Then CodeIntegrity

The servicing history explains some of the odd edges.

### 10.1 26100.7309 (December 2025): A Signed Empty Report

The API, the NT path and the VTL1 producer ship together, but `SkpBuildAuthenticatedRuntimeReports` calls nothing. For bit 0 it emits a constant 16-byte driver report: `ReportType 0`, `ReportSize 16`, `NumberOfDrivers 0`, flags 0, and a DWORD 1 at +0x0C that no SDK field explains. The rest of the package, header, nonce, digest and PSS signature, is built exactly as on 9711. So from 7309 to 7623 every machine with VBS running returned a validly signed report saying "no drivers".

### 10.2 26100.7705 (January 2026): The Table Arrives

The Secure Kernel gains the driver table and the recording path, `skci` gains `SkciCalculateImageAttestationData`, and `skci!SkciTransferVersionResource` gains the active-policy check on the version resource. The NT side changes in two ways: the secure call number moves, and the kernel now reads the output length once and refuses to copy more than that. kernelbase drops the size-query special case. From here on an empty table produces `STATUS_NOT_SUPPORTED`, never a signed empty report. 26100.7705 to 26100.9711 changes only the secure call number on the NT side.

That combination creates a downgrade case. The package carries no OS build, and only the 7309 to 7623 builds ever sign a well-formed driver report with zero drivers (a later build can sign a malformed, zeroed header, section 13.1). A verifier that rejects `NumberOfDrivers == 0` cannot be fooled by a machine held at an old update into reporting "nothing loaded"; one that treats zero entries as clean can be.

### 10.3 28000.3231 (26H1): The CodeIntegrity Report

On 26H1, kernelbase, NT and VTL1 accept bits 0 and 1, and bit 1 appends a CodeIntegrity report built by `skci!SkciBuildAttestationReport`. It does not describe drivers. It is a snapshot of the code integrity state, built when a policy is committed, not when the report is requested. The snapshot is one generation (`NumberOfGenerations` is always 1, although the SDK allows more) with a `CommitTime` taken from the Secure Kernel's reference time, a since-boot counter rather than wall-clock time. Its records reuse the Measured Boot event codes: first `SIPAEVENT_OS_REVOCATION_LIST` with the driver blocklist's creation time and SHA-256 digest, then the active base and supplemental policies with their signers. For a verifier that is the more interesting report, since it says which blocklist and which policies are enforced right now. The same build also clears the sticky Unloaded flag when a hash is validated again, but it looks the hash up before taking the table lock and does not look again afterwards. Two concurrent 0x1D calls for the same new hash could insert it twice. In the 9711 NT code the Mm load lock is released around driver section creation, so it does not serialize 0x1D; loads of the same file share one validation, but two files with identical bytes could race. I did not trace the 28000 NT side.

The SDK preview 10.0.29648 adds a third type, `RuntimeReportTypeHotpatch = 2`, which Trehan's post describes and which needs an Insider build. None of the builds I analyzed accepts bit 2.

---

## 11. On a Real Machine

Static analysis predicts; a real machine says whether the predictions hold. Unless a section says otherwise, the experiments above ran on my own desktop: Windows 11 26H2 (binaries 10.0.26100.9711), an AMD Ryzen 9 5900X with a firmware TPM 2.0, Secure Boot on, VBS on, and Memory Integrity toggled per test. The exceptions ran in Hyper-V lab VMs: the WinDbg trace (section 3.4), the file recopy test (section 8.2), the long OEM name (section 13.1) and the VTL1 reads (section 15). A small C harness calls the API and the raw syscall across a parameter matrix and inventories the loaded modules; a Python parser checks every package field against the SDK layout, recomputes the SHA-512 digests, pulls the IDKS from the TCG log and verifies the RSA-PSS signature. I wrote down the expected result before each run, so a wrong one would count. Several were wrong. I expected reloading the Process Explorer driver to push its LoadCount to 2, and it stayed 1, which is what sent me to the cached-control-area path in section 8.2. I expected the signing key to be reused across ordinary reboots, and it was reused only when the masked PCRs matched (section 5.4). In the lab VM I expected a test-signed driver to get no OEM name, and it got one (section 13.1). The rest held, and the results sit in the sections above where they belong.

Some planned experiments did not run on this machine. Loading a known-vulnerable or blocklisted driver on a daily-use machine opens a local privilege escalation path for as long as it is loaded, and it would exercise the same recording path the Process Explorer load already did. The test-signing, debugging and report-poisoning experiments need test-signing or debug mode, and on this machine `bcdedit` refuses both `testsigning on` and `debug on`: "The value is protected by Secure Boot policy and cannot be modified or deleted." Turning Secure Boot off would not help either. This machine's VBS configuration requires Secure Boot, and from my reading of winload, VBS is then simply not started for that boot. There would be no report to test. The next section comes back to what that means. Kernel debugging and test-signing are blocked on this machine for the same reason, so the live WinDbg trace in section 3.4 and the long OEM name test in section 13.1 ran in Hyper-V virtual machines instead.

---

## 12. Using the Report as a Defender

The report is worth having: it gives a remote party a list of runtime drivers that a kernel attacker cannot rewrite after the fact. But it proves nothing unless it is verified completely, and even a fully verified report is blind to some things by design. Here is how to use it, and where it stops.

Verify it completely. The checks, in order:

1. Verify a TPM quote with your own nonce, replay the TCG log against the quoted PCRs, and from the replayed log require HVCI (`SIPAEVENT_VBS_HVCI_POLICY = 1`) and reject test-signing and debugging. Without HVCI nothing is recorded and the API returns `ERROR_NOT_SUPPORTED`, so a missing report is a finding, not a pass.
2. Take the IDKS from `SIPAEVENT_VSM_IDKS_INFO` in the boot segment of the log, and anchor it with the boot-time quote. A hibernate-resume segment has no IDKS event, and a post-resume quote no longer matches the boot segment, so neither can vouch for the key.
3. Issue a fresh, single-use, server-side nonce for each report and check it in the package. The key alone does not prove the package came from the current boot: on a machine with a hardware TPM it survives every reboot that leaves the sealed PCRs alone, so an earlier boot's package verifies with today's key, and the nonce is copied verbatim and never checked for reuse.
4. Check magic `RTRP`, version 1, the bitmap, digest type and signature scheme, verify RSA-PSS with SHA-512 and a 64-byte salt over the first `72 + 68*N` bytes, and recompute every report digest.
5. Parse defensively: a driver report that does not parse is malformed, not empty (section 13.1), and `NumberOfDrivers == 0` should never be accepted from a current build, since only the 26100.7309-to-7623 era ever signed a well-formed empty report.
6. Treat `ReportOverflowed` as an incomplete list; it means the 384-image table filled, and past that point drivers load without being listed. Never drop entries with `Unloaded = 1`: it means "unloaded at least once since boot", not "gone now", and it is tracked per image hash. Key on the image hash. The signer thumbprint is often just Microsoft's hardware publisher certificate, shared by every driver signed through that program, and `InternalName` and the OEM name are labels, not identity.
7. Get boot-start drivers from the TCG log; the report never has them.

Then there is what even a perfectly verified report cannot see. These are not bugs; they are the shape of the feature.

- **Allowed but vulnerable drivers look like any other entry.** An entry is identity only: hash, thumbprint, names, LoadCount, flags. The blocklist is applied by CI at load time, and a driver it allows is recorded like any other. The useful part is that it stays listed after it unloads.
- **Post-load exploitation, data-only attacks and DMA.** The list changes only when a new kernel image is validated or a recorded image's range is released. Exploiting an already loaded driver, tampering with kernel data, removing callbacks or device DMA never touch it.
- **Hotpatched code.** The HotPatch flag marks a patch image; applying a patch to a driver changes nothing in that driver's entry.
- **Test-signing and debugging are not in the package;** only the TCG log's events record them, which is why step 1 reads them from the quoted log. On a machine whose VBS configuration does not require Secure Boot, a report can even be produced with a kernel debugger attached, visible only as `SIPAEVENT_OSKERNELDEBUG = 1` in the log.

Correlating report entries with endpoint telemetry takes one extra step. Defender XDR's `DeviceEvents` table and Sysmon's driver-load event document only file hashes (SHA-1, SHA-256, MD5, and IMPHASH for Sysmon). The report identifies a driver by its Authenticode PE hash instead, so the agent has to compute that hash for the loaded files and upload the mapping. On my machine every loaded driver's report hash matched its file's Authenticode PE hash, so the mapping works. The research artifacts include KQL queries for Defender XDR and Sentinel built on that mapping, and Sigma rules for the weakening steps (HVCI disabled in the registry, `bcdedit` changes). I have not run the queries against a live tenant.

---

## 13. Bugs and Sharp Edges

None of these lets VTL0 forge or alter a report. They are places where a careless verifier, or an unusual signer, gets surprising results.

### 13.1 A Long OEM Name Leaves a Signed but Broken Report

I traced this statically and then reproduced it on the lab VM. `SkpBuildNtDriversReport` converts each OEM name to UTF-8 in a 256-byte scratch buffer. `skci` does not cap the name on the way in; it keeps up to about 64 KB, so a name longer than 256 UTF-8 bytes is reachable. When one is reached in the fill pass, `RtlUnicodeToUTF8N` returns `STATUS_BUFFER_TOO_SMALL` and the function returns that status without writing the driver report header or its size. The outer builder, `SkpBuildAuthenticatedRuntimeReports`, treats `STATUS_BUFFER_TOO_SMALL` as "size-only pass, keep counting", adds the unchanged byte count and returns success, and `SkProduceRuntimeSignedReport` signs a package whose driver report header was never written.

Report generation is not blocked. Because entries are never removed, any fill request that reaches the long-named entry for the rest of that boot returns a signed but malformed package, with a driver report header of all zeros and a digest that is the SHA-512 of zero bytes, so it covers nothing. The entries built before the failure sit inside the package but outside every digest. A verifier that checks the signature and the digests and then treats an unparseable driver report as "no drivers" would be blinded. To reproduce it I signed two throwaway drivers with a self-signed certificate on the test-signing lab VM: one with a short program name and one whose `signtool /d` description is 300 bytes, which becomes the OPUS program name. The control driver confirmed something I had wrong: a test-signed driver *does* get its OEM name captured. It appeared in the report as `rtattest E14 control`, with an image hash equal to the file's Authenticode hash, in a clean 96-entry report. I had assumed the signing level would bar the capture; it does not, which is the only reason this experiment works at all.

The long-named driver then did exactly what the trace predicted. The package came back structurally intact, magic `RTRP`, version 1, `PackageSize` matching the file, a 256-byte signature in place, but its driver report header read `ReportType 0`, `ReportSize 0`, `NumberOfDrivers 0`, with the real entry bytes still sitting in the buffer behind the zeroed header. Walking the VTL1 table from the host (section 15.4) with both drivers loaded showed the root cause directly: the table had grown to 97 entries, and the long driver's entry held its full 300-byte program name. So `skci` does store the name uncapped; the overflow is only in the report builder's 256-byte scratch buffer, never in the table. The report does not parse. A larger buffer (required + 4096) produced the same zeroed header, so it is not a sizing artifact. The one thing I could not check on this VM is the signature itself: the VM has no vTPM, so there is no measured IDKS to verify against (section 5.2). The 256-byte signature is present in every package; I just cannot anchor it to a measurement here.

The defense on the verifier side is cheap regardless: a driver report whose `ReportSize` is smaller than its 12-byte header, or whose entries do not fit inside it, is malformed and must fail verification, not be read as "no drivers".

### 13.2 Smaller Edges

- **OEM name sizing (from the code, not tested).** The size pass reserves one byte per UTF-16 unit of the OEM name, but the fill writes UTF-8, which is longer for non-ASCII names. With a buffer of exactly the queried size, a later driver whose estimate no longer fits is skipped (zero slot, still counted), or the call fails with `STATUS_INTERNAL_ERROR`. With a larger buffer, the extra bytes fall outside `ReportSize` and therefore outside the digest. Allocating a little more than the queried size avoids the first problem.
- **InternalName** is copied without a NUL when it is exactly 32 bytes, and cut to 31 bytes, possibly inside a UTF-8 sequence, when longer.
- **Unknown hash algorithms.** An image whose hash ALG_ID is not one of the four SHA variants gets hash length 0, and all such images would collapse into one entry. I could not establish whether `skci` ever produces such an ALG_ID for a validated kernel image.
- **Big buffers.** A `*ReportBufferSize` above about 2 MB fails with `ERROR_INVALID_PARAMETER`.
- **Unloaded flags mid-report.** They can change while a report is serialized (section 7.3).

---

## 14. What I Could Not Verify

A few things in this post stayed open. Over time several of the original ones closed, so this list is shorter than it was; what follows is what I still cannot show.

- Whether a driver hotpatch is recorded as its own entry. The VTL1 path records it (section 8.4), and walking the live table (section 15.4) would have shown it directly, but no hotpatchable image was loaded on the lab VM, so its cleared HotPatch bit proves nothing. The exact validation flags CI passes for a patch image, and a live test with a real hotpatch, are still open.
- Kernel Soft Reboot, two steps short. The old Secure Kernel's key export, the driver table being left behind, and the loading step in `skksrext.sys` are traced (section 8.6). What is still open is how the successor receives the exported keys, and whether the loader image that `skksrext` starts goes on to load a fresh Secure Kernel. Nothing in `skksrext` shows either, five service slots it calls are unnamed, and I have no machine that performs a KSR to watch it.
- The CodeIntegrity report, only statically, from the 26H1 (28000) binaries; the 26H2 media I had still runs the 26100 Secure Kernel, which does not build it.
- Tracing VTL1 execution in software. I can now read VTL1 memory from the host, walk the driver table and match every field against the static layout (section 15), but that is a snapshot, not a trace. A VTL0 debugger stops at the boundary. The way Connor did it is a hardware debugger over Intel DCI (SourcePoint), which needs an Intel CPU; this machine is AMD.
- One signature I could not anchor. The long-OEM-name package (section 13.1) was reproduced on a lab VM with no vTPM, so there was no measured IDKS to verify its 256-byte signature against (section 5.2). The package is structurally valid and signed; I just could not check the signature cryptographically on that machine.

---

## 15. Reading VTL1 from the Host

An earlier draft of this post ended by saying the Secure Kernel's memory was unreadable from the host. The WinHv interface returned zeros where securekernel should be, my attempt to attach a hypervisor debugger to the guest went nowhere, and the one tool I found that reads VTL1 memory from the host needs a driver with a revoked certificate that my HVCI desktop refused to load. That last part pointed at the answer: if the blocker was my own host's security, the experiment could run from a host whose security I was willing to take apart.

### 15.1 The Setup

The target is a Hyper-V virtual machine running the same 26100.9711 build with VBS and HVCI on. It is not the VM from section 3.4; I built a second one for this, without a virtual TPM. The tool is [LiveCloudKd](https://github.com/gerhart01/LiveCloudKd){:target="_blank"} v3.3.2 by gerhart01, a Hyper-V memory introspection toolkit whose SDK (`hvlib.dll`) and Python wrapper expose two read interfaces. `ReadInterfaceWinHv` (method 2) asks the hypervisor for guest memory with the `HvReadGpa` hypercall; in my runs it saw VTL0 memory only. `ReadInterfaceHvmmDrvInternal` (method 1) loads `hvmm.sys`, a kernel driver signed with a revoked Atheros certificate, which, in the README's words, reads "data directly from kernel memory" using undocumented Hyper-V structures, without going through the hypercall. That direct path also returns the guest's VTL1 pages. I have not reversed `hvmm.sys`, so I cannot say exactly how. To inspect VTL1 isolation you have to defeat it on the machine doing the inspecting.

I tore down the host for this. Secure Boot was off in firmware and test signing on (`bcdedit`), Memory Integrity was off, and I loaded `hvmm.sys` with `sc create` and `sc start`. That was not enough: with the host's own Secure Kernel running, every read through the driver came back as zeros, even though Group Policy already had VBS set to Disabled. What made it work was `bcdedit /set vsmlaunchtype Off`, which stops the host's VTL1 from launching while leaving the hypervisor, and so the guests, running. The guest kept its own VBS and HVCI running throughout. All reads ran from a Python script on the host, calling `hvlib.dll` through ctypes.

### 15.2 Finding the Secure Kernel

A brute-force scan reads guest physical memory in 16 MB blocks through `SdkReadPhysicalMemory` (`ReadPhysicalMemoryBlock` in the Python wrapper), checking page-aligned offsets for an MZ/PE header. The Secure Kernel is identified by its SizeOfImage (0x175000 for the 9711 build), a kernel-range ImageBase above `0xfffff80000000000`, and at least ten sections. An earlier run without those last two filters picked up a false positive at GPA `0x4ff000` that had a user-mode ImageBase and three sections. The scan found `securekernel.exe` at GPA `0x16d1000` with TimeDateStamp `0x88c2fa26`, matching the on-disk binary. `ntoskrnl` sat at GPA `0x4b62000`.

### 15.3 Reading Runtime Globals

Physical reads work for the PE header because the image's first pages happen to be physically contiguous with it. The `.data` section is a different story. `SkmiFlags` and the driver table counters live there, and reading the header's GPA plus their RVA returned zeros, so those pages are not where a flat offset from the header says they should be.

The SDK already solves this. `SdkReadVirtualMemory` takes a virtual address and does the translation itself. These addresses are mapped only in VTL1's page tables, so that is the translation it must be doing, and it handles the page scatter in a single call. One operational detail: whatever state `hvmm.sys` builds for a session goes stale between script invocations, so the driver must be stopped and restarted between runs, or every read in a session must finish before cleanup.

![Resolving a VTL1 global two ways](/assets/img/posts/rtattest-vtl1-reads.svg)
_Why a physical read is not enough. The image header and the start of `.text` sit in one contiguous block, but the `.data` page holding `SkmiFlags` is somewhere else, so only a translation through VTL1's own page tables finds it._

I pointed the virtual reads at the RVAs from the IDA analysis in sections 6 and 7:

| what | RVA | IDA prediction | live read |
|---|---|---|---|
| `SkpNtDriversTable` count | 0x1324B8 | runtime value, section 7.1 | 95 (0x5F) |
| `SkpNtDriversTable` capacity | 0x1324C0 | 384, section 7.1 | 384 (0x180) |
| `SkmiFlags` | 0x13CB28 | bit 0x10 = HVCI, section 6.2 | 0x40f1b7, bit 0x10 set |

The capacity of 384 is the value `SkmmInitSystem` passes and the SDK calls `MaximumDriversRecorded`. The 95 entries fit the WinDbg trace from section 3.4 too, although that ran a day earlier in the other VM: its report had 97 entries, the same 95 driver names plus `serial.sys` and `serenum.sys`, the serial port drivers, presumably loaded there because a COM port was attached for the debugger. `SkmiFlags` bit 0x10 being set confirms the HVCI gate in section 6.2 was open, which is why the table had entries at all.

As a sanity check I read the first 64 bytes of `.text` through both physical memory (the contiguous block at the image GPA) and virtual memory (the page-table walk). They were byte-identical:

```
cc cc cc cc cc cc cc cc  48 ba 00 00 00 00 00 40
00 00 48 85 15 77 67 12  00 74 17 48 8b 05 76 67
12 00 48 23 c2 48 3b 05  6c 67 12 00 75 04 b0 01
c3 cc 32 c0 c3 cc cc cc  cc cc cc cc 4c 8b dc 4d
```

The `cc` padding (INT3) and the code in between match IDA's view of securekernel's `.text` entry.

### 15.4 Walking the Table

Reading the counters shows the globals are populated. Reading the entries tests the layout. The bucket array pointer at RVA 0x1324B0 held `0xffffd1000281e5b0`, so from there it is a matter of walking the bucket array, following each chain link at +0x00, and decoding the eleven fields section 7.1 lists. My script walked 384 bucket slots, one per possible entry, which overshoots: the lookup code takes the real bucket count from a separate field and masks the hash with it. The slots past the real array held unrelated pointers whose "entries" failed basic sanity checks and were dropped.

On this freshly booted guest the walk produced exactly 95 entries, the same number as the count field, with insertion indices running 0 to 94 with no gaps. That last part matters: section 7.3 says the report comes out in load order because each entry is written into the slot its insertion index names, and a contiguous run from zero is what that requires. For all 95 entries the bucket key at +0x08 equals the first 8 bytes of the image hash reached through the pointer at +0x20, which is the keying rule section 7.1 describes, checked against live memory rather than inferred from the lookup code.

```
idx  InternalName        LoadCount  image hash alg  flags
0    crashdmp.sys        1          CALG_SHA_256    -
1    diskdump.sys        1          CALG_SHA_256    Unloaded
2    storvsc.sys         1          CALG_SHA_256    Unloaded
3    vmbkmcl.sys         1          CALG_SHA_256    Unloaded
4    dumpfve.sys         1          CALG_SHA_256    Unloaded
5    cdrom.sys           1          CALG_SHA1       -
...
```

The per-image algorithm split is real: 53 entries were SHA-256 and 42 SHA-1, while the thumbprint ALG_ID was `CALG_SHA1` on all 95, each with a 20 byte thumbprint. That is section 7.2's "always SHA-1, whatever the image hash algorithm", confirmed.

Every entry had LoadCount 1, and the BootDriver and HotPatch bits were clear on all 95, which is what section 7.3 predicts for BootDriver since nothing writes it. `Unloaded` was set on seven, four of them the crash-dump copies the report lists under their original names. One entry carried a zero-length `InternalName` with a perfectly good hash and thumbprint, so the advice not to key on that field is not theoretical. None carried an OEM name, which fits a guest whose drivers are all inbox Microsoft images.

One correction to make here, because it caught me. The count is not the qword at RVA 0x1324B8. The live value there was `0x4b0000005f`: the low DWORD is 95 and the high DWORD held an unrelated `0x4b`. Reading the whole qword gives 322,122,547,295 drivers. The Secure Kernel itself only ever casts that qword to `unsigned int` before comparing it against the capacity DWORD, which is why the stray high half does no harm in the code and does plenty of harm to anyone reading the field from outside.

No hotpatchable image was loaded on this guest, so its HotPatch bit tells me nothing about whether a patch image gets its own entry. That question stays where section 8.4 left it.

### 15.5 The Isolation Contrast

The same script then switched to the WinHv interface and read the same guest physical addresses. At ntoskrnl's GPA, WinHv returned a valid MZ header: VTL0 memory is visible. At securekernel's GPA, it returned 64 bytes of zeros.

![Reading VTL1 from the host, two ways](/assets/img/posts/rtattest-slat-bypass.svg)
_The same two guest-physical addresses read through both interfaces. The WinHv hypercall path returns `ntoskrnl` in VTL0 and nothing but zeros at securekernel's GPA. `hvmm.sys` reads the guest's memory directly from the host kernel and gets the image in full._

That is VTL1 isolation doing what the background section describes. The guest's VTL1 appears to be provided by the host's Hyper-V: the Measured Boot logs of the section 3.4 VM, built the same way apart from its virtual TPM, record `securekernel.exe` and `skci.dll` but no hypervisor image, while my host's log records `hvloader` and `hvax64`. The hypervisor's documented read path hands back the Secure Kernel's pages as zeros. `hvmm.sys` gets past that because it does not ask the hypervisor at all: it reads from the host kernel side, which on my host only worked with the host's own protections switched off.

### 15.6 Reproducing This

1. Set up a Hyper-V Generation 2 VM running Windows 11 24H2 (26100.x) with VBS and HVCI enabled in the registry: `EnableVirtualizationBasedSecurity = 1` and `RequirePlatformSecurityFeatures = 0` under `HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\DeviceGuard`, and `Enabled = 1` under its `Scenarios\HypervisorEnforcedCodeIntegrity` subkey. My VMs also had nested virtualization exposed (`Set-VMProcessor -ExposeVirtualizationExtensions $true`); I did not test whether that is needed.

2. On the host, turn off Secure Boot in firmware, enable test signing (`bcdedit /set testsigning on`), turn Memory Integrity off, and stop the host's own VTL1 from launching with `bcdedit /set vsmlaunchtype Off`. Reboot. This opens the host to unsigned kernel drivers, so do it on a lab machine.

3. Download [LiveCloudKd v3.3.2](https://github.com/gerhart01/LiveCloudKd){:target="_blank"} by gerhart01. `hvlib.dll` and `hvmm.sys` are in the release root, and the Python wrapper is `sdk/hvlib.py`. Load the driver:

   ```
   sc create hvmm binPath= "C:\path\to\hvmm.sys" type= kernel
   sc start hvmm
   ```

4. Use `ReadInterfaceHvmmDrvInternal` for VTL1 reads. `ReadInterfaceWinHv` saw only VTL0 for me and is useful as a contrast. For globals in `.data`, call `SdkReadVirtualMemory` (`ReadVirtualMemoryBlock` in the Python wrapper), not `SdkReadPhysicalMemory`, because those pages are not physically contiguous with the image header.

5. Stop and restart `hvmm.sys` (`sc stop hvmm` then `sc start hvmm`) between separate script invocations, or do all reads in one session. The driver's state goes stale as soon as a process calls `cleanup`, and the next process gets `ReadPhysicalMemoryBlock error` on every read. I lost a run to exactly this.

6. Do not trust `Win32_DeviceGuard.VirtualizationBasedSecurityStatus` as the readiness check. On my Hyper-V host it kept reporting 2 ("running") with host VTL1 switched off and the reads working, presumably because the hypervisor itself is still up. The check that worked for me is a scan: when the bypass was live, a few hundred PE images turned up in guest physical memory; when it was not, every block read as zeros.

7. When finished, remove the override (`bcdedit /deletevalue vsmlaunchtype`), disable test signing, re-enable Secure Boot in firmware, turn Memory Integrity back on, and reboot.

---

## Conclusion

The Runtime Attestation Report is not a TPM hash chain. It is a list that the Secure Kernel keeps in VTL1 memory, one entry per kernel image that passed VTL1 validation since boot, never pruned, and signed on request with a key that winload generated or unsealed at boot and measured into the TCG log. A live read of VTL1 from the host confirmed the table at the exact IDA offsets, with the HVCI gate set and 95 drivers recorded (section 15). That is a different design from what Riot described, but it delivers the property Riot wanted: a kernel attacker in VTL0 can load and unload a vulnerable driver, but cannot make the Secure Kernel forget it.

What surprised me most is how much of the security sits with the verifier. It needs HVCI or there is no report at all, `Unloaded` does not mean unloaded, and on bare metal the signing key quietly survives every reboot that leaves the sealed PCRs alone, so an old package keeps verifying until a nonce policy says otherwise. None of that is a bug. It is what the report is, and a verifier that does not know it will accept things it should not.

If I continue this, the next steps are the CodeIntegrity report on 26H1, which says which blocklist and policies are enforced right now, and the hotpatch report on the Insider builds. Both use the same package and the same key, so most of this post should carry over.

---

`_tabs/about.md`:

```md
---
# the default layout is 'page'
icon: fas fa-info-circle
order: 4
---

I'm s4dbrd. I spend most of my time reversing Windows kernel drivers, looking for vulnerabilities, and figuring out how to exploit them. Kernel research has always been what I'm most passionate about. I also enjoy reverse engineering in general and have done some work on anti-cheat systems along the way.

This blog is where I document what I find and what I learn along the way. I'm still learning, every driver I open teaches me something new. If anything here helps you or if you spot something wrong, I'd love to hear about it!

You can find me on [X](https://x.com/s4dbrd) or [LinkedIn](https://www.linkedin.com/in/s4dbrd/).

```

`tools/run.sh`:

```sh
#!/usr/bin/env bash
#
# Run jekyll serve and then launch the site

prod=false
command="bundle exec jekyll s -l"
host="127.0.0.1"

help() {
  echo "Usage:"
  echo
  echo "   bash /path/to/run [options]"
  echo
  echo "Options:"
  echo "     -H, --host [HOST]    Host to bind to."
  echo "     -p, --production     Run Jekyll in 'production' mode."
  echo "     -h, --help           Print this help information."
}

while (($#)); do
  opt="$1"
  case $opt in
  -H | --host)
    host="$2"
    shift 2
    ;;
  -p | --production)
    prod=true
    shift
    ;;
  -h | --help)
    help
    exit 0
    ;;
  *)
    echo -e "> Unknown option: '$opt'\n"
    help
    exit 1
    ;;
  esac
done

command="$command -H $host"

if $prod; then
  command="JEKYLL_ENV=production $command"
fi

if [ -e /proc/1/cgroup ] && grep -q docker /proc/1/cgroup; then
  command="$command --force_polling"
fi

echo -e "\n> $command\n"
eval "$command"

```

`tools/test.sh`:

```sh
#!/usr/bin/env bash
#
# Build and test the site content
#
# Requirement: html-proofer, jekyll
#
# Usage: See help information

set -eu

SITE_DIR="_site"

_config="_config.yml"

_baseurl=""

help() {
  echo "Build and test the site content"
  echo
  echo "Usage:"
  echo
  echo "   bash $0 [options]"
  echo
  echo "Options:"
  echo '     -c, --config   "<config_a[,config_b[...]]>"    Specify config file(s)'
  echo "     -h, --help               Print this information."
}

read_baseurl() {
  if [[ $_config == *","* ]]; then
    # multiple config
    IFS=","
    read -ra config_array <<<"$_config"

    # reverse loop the config files
    for ((i = ${#config_array[@]} - 1; i >= 0; i--)); do
      _tmp_baseurl="$(grep '^baseurl:' "${config_array[i]}" | sed "s/.*: *//;s/['\"]//g;s/#.*//")"

      if [[ -n $_tmp_baseurl ]]; then
        _baseurl="$_tmp_baseurl"
        break
      fi
    done

  else
    # single config
    _baseurl="$(grep '^baseurl:' "$_config" | sed "s/.*: *//;s/['\"]//g;s/#.*//")"
  fi
}

main() {
  # clean up
  if [[ -d $SITE_DIR ]]; then
    rm -rf "$SITE_DIR"
  fi

  read_baseurl

  # build
  JEKYLL_ENV=production bundle exec jekyll b \
    -d "$SITE_DIR$_baseurl" -c "$_config"

  # test
  bundle exec htmlproofer "$SITE_DIR" \
    --disable-external \
    --ignore-urls "/^http:\/\/127.0.0.1/,/^http:\/\/0.0.0.0/,/^http:\/\/localhost/"
}

while (($#)); do
  opt="$1"
  case $opt in
  -c | --config)
    _config="$2"
    shift
    shift
    ;;
  -h | --help)
    help
    exit 0
    ;;
  *)
    # unknown option
    help
    exit 1
    ;;
  esac
done

main

```
